k=\vartheta_{p,q}:=\min\left\{\frac{p(N-2)}{N-2p},\,\frac{q(N-2)}{Nq-2q-dq+d}\right\}.

(3.61)

Using this selection together with (
2.5
) and (
2.6
), from (
3.57
), (
3.58
), and (
3.60
), we get that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}fv_{{}_{m}}\,dx\,\leq\,C\|f\|_{{}_{p,\Omega}}\|w_{{}_{m}}\|^{{}^{\frac{2(\vartheta_{p,q}-1)}{\vartheta_{p,q}}}}_{{}_{\frac{2N}{N-2},\Omega}}\leq\,c^{{}^{\frac{2(\vartheta_{p,q}-1)}{\vartheta_{p,q}}}}_{1}C\|f\|_{{}_{p,\Omega}}\|w_{{}_{m}}\|^{{}^{\frac{2(\vartheta_{p,q}-1)}{\vartheta_{p,q}}}}_{{}_{\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)}},

(3.62)

and

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}gv_{{}_{m}}\,d\mu\,\leq\,C^{\prime}\|g\|_{{}_{q,\Gamma}}\|w_{{}_{m}}\|^{{}^{\frac{2(\vartheta_{p,q}-1)}{\vartheta_{p,q}}}}_{{}_{\frac{2d}{N-2},\Gamma}}\leq\,c^{{}^{\frac{2(\vartheta_{p,q}-1)}{\vartheta_{p,q}}}}_{2}C^{\prime}\|g\|_{{}_{q,\Gamma}}\|w_{{}_{m}}\|^{{}^{\frac{2(\vartheta_{p,q}-1)}{\vartheta_{p,q}}}}_{{}_{\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)}},

(3.63)

and

\|w_{{}_{m}}\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega}}\,\leq\,c^{{}^{\frac{2(\vartheta_{p,q}-1)}{\vartheta_{p,q}}}}_{1}C^{\prime\prime}\|u^{+}\|_{{}_{2,\Omega}}\|w_{{}_{m}}\|^{{}^{\frac{2(\vartheta_{p,q}-1)}{\vartheta_{p,q}}}}_{{}_{\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)}}+\epsilon\,\|w_{{}_{m}}\|^{2}_{{}_{\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)}},

(3.64)

for some constants
C,\,C^{\prime},\,C^{\prime\prime}>0
. Selecting
\epsilon>0
appropriately, and combining (
3.56
), (
3.62
), (
3.63
),
and (
3.64
), we get the existence of a constant
C_{0}>0
, such that

\|w_{{}_{m}}\|^{2}_{{}_{\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)}}\,\leq\,C_{0}\left(\|f\|_{{}_{p,\Omega}}+\|g\|_{{}_{q,\Gamma}}+\|u^{+}\|_{{}_{2,\Omega}}\right)\|w_{{}_{m}}\|^{{}^{\frac{2(\vartheta_{p,q}-1)}{\vartheta_{p,q}}}}_{{}_{\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)}},

from where we arrive at

\left\|w^{{}^{\frac{2}{\vartheta_{p,q}}}}_{{}_{m}}\right\|_{{}_{\frac{N\vartheta_{p,q}}{N-2},\Omega}}+\left\|w^{{}^{\frac{2}{\vartheta_{p,q}}}}_{{}_{m}}\right\|_{{}_{\frac{d\vartheta_{p,q}}{N-2},\Gamma}}\,\leq\,C_{0}\left(\|f\|_{{}_{p,\Omega}}+\|g\|_{{}_{q,\Gamma}}+\|u^{+}\|_{{}_{2,\Omega}}\right).

(3.65)

Since the sequence
w^{{}^{\frac{2}{\vartheta_{p,q}}}}_{{}_{m}}:=\left[\psi_{{}_{\frac{\vartheta_{p,q}}{2},m}}(u)\right]^{{}^{\frac{2}{\vartheta_{p,q}}}}

(for the function
\psi_{{}_{t,m}}(\cdot)
given by (
3.28
))
is increasing with
w^{{}^{\frac{2}{\vartheta_{p,q}}}}_{{}_{m}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle m\rightarrow\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}u^{+}
, and application of
the Monotone Convergence Theorem of Lebesgue in (
3.65
) gives

|\|\textbf{u}^{+}\||_{{}_{\frac{N\vartheta_{p,q}}{N-2},\frac{d\vartheta_{p,q}}{N-2}}}\,\leq\,C_{0}\left(|\|(f,g)\||_{{}_{p,q}}+\|u^{+}\|_{{}_{2,\Omega}}\right).

(3.66)

This completes the proof of the first case of the theorem.

\bullet

Case 2:

p<N/2<\frac{d}{d+2-N}\geq q
, or

q<\frac{d}{d+2-N}
and
N/p\leq p<q
.

For this case, we put

k:=\varsigma_{p}:=\frac{p(N-2)}{Np-2p-dp+d}.

(3.67)

Inserting this in (
3.57
), (
3.58
), and (
3.60
), and using the fact that
N-2<d<N
and
q^{\prime}<p^{\prime}
, we get that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}fv_{{}_{m}}\,dx\,\leq\,\|f\|_{{}_{p,\Omega}}\|w_{{}_{m}}\|^{{}^{\frac{2(\varsigma_{p}-1)}{\varsigma_{p}}}}_{{}_{\frac{2d}{N-2},\Omega}}\leq\,c^{{}^{\frac{2(\varsigma_{p}-1)}{\varsigma_{p}}}}_{1}C_{1}\|f\|_{{}_{p,\Omega}}\|w_{{}_{m}}\|^{{}^{\frac{2(\varsigma_{p}-1)}{\varsigma_{p}}}}_{{}_{\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)}},

and

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}gv_{{}_{m}}\,d\mu\,\leq\,\|g\|_{{}_{q,\Gamma}}\|w_{{}_{m}}\|^{{}^{\frac{2(\varsigma_{p}-1)}{\varsigma_{p}}}}_{{}_{\left(\frac{q}{q-1}\right)\frac{2(\varsigma_{p}-1)}{\varsigma_{p}},\Gamma}}\,\leq\,C^{\prime}_{1}\|g\|_{{}_{q,\Gamma}}\|w_{{}_{m}}\|^{{}^{\frac{2(\varsigma_{p}-1)}{\varsigma_{p}}}}_{{}_{\frac{2d}{N-2},\Gamma}}\leq\,c^{{}^{\frac{2(\varsigma_{p}-1)}{\varsigma_{p}}}}_{2}C^{\prime\prime}_{1}\|g\|_{{}_{q,\Gamma}}\|w_{{}_{m}}\|^{{}^{\frac{2(\varsigma_{p}-1)}{\varsigma_{p}}}}_{{}_{\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)}},

and

\|w_{{}_{m}}\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega}}\,\leq\,c^{{}^{\frac{2(\varsigma_{p}-1)}{\varsigma_{p}}}}_{1}\|u^{+}\|_{{}_{2,\Omega}}\|w_{{}_{m}}\|c^{{}^{\frac{2(\varsigma_{p}-1)}{\varsigma_{p}}}}_{{}_{\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)}}+\epsilon\,\|w_{{}_{m}}\|^{2}_{{}_{\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)}},

for some constants
C_{1},\,C^{\prime}_{1},\,C^{\prime\prime}_{1}>0
. Proceeding in the exact way as the previous case, we have

|\|\textbf{u}^{+}\||_{{}_{\frac{N\varsigma_{p}}{N-2},\frac{d\varsigma_{p}}{N-2}}}\,\leq\,C^{\prime}_{0}\left(|\|(f,g)\||_{{}_{p,q}}+\|u^{+}\|_{{}_{2,\Omega}}\right)

(3.68)

for some positive constant
C_{0}>0
, which is the desired conclusion for the second case.

\bullet

Case 3:

q<\frac{d}{d+2-N}
and
p\geq\max\{N/2,q\}
.

Letting

k:=\varsigma_{q}:=\frac{q(N-2)}{Nq-2q-dq+d},

(3.69)

this case follows in the same way as in the previous case; we deduce the existence of a constant
C^{\prime\prime}_{0}>0
such that

|\|\textbf{u}^{+}\||_{{}_{\frac{N\varsigma_{q}}{N-2},\frac{d\varsigma_{q}}{N-2}}}\,\leq\,C^{\prime\prime}_{0}\left(|\|(f,g)\||_{{}_{p,q}}+\|u^{+}\|_{{}_{2,\Omega}}\right).

(3.70)

\bullet

Case 4:

p=N/2
and
q\geq\frac{d}{d+2-N}
, or

q=\frac{d}{d+2-N}
and
p\geq N/2
.

We will prove the case when
p=N/2
and
q\geq\frac{d}{d+2-N}
; the other situation follows in an analogous manner.
Given
r>2^{\ast}_{{}_{N}}
,
s>2^{\ast}_{{}_{d}}
arbitrarily fixed, put
\tau_{r,s}:=\max\{r,s,N\}
.
Then there exists
p_{0}\in[(2^{\ast}_{{}_{N}})^{\prime},N/2)
such that

f\in L^{p_{0}}(\Omega)\indent\indent\textrm{and}\indent\indent\frac{p_{0}}{N-2p_{0}}=\frac{\tau_{r,s}}{d}.

Given
r>2^{\ast}_{{}_{N}}
,
s>2^{\ast}_{{}_{d}}
arbitrarily fixed, put
\tau_{r,s}:=\max\{r,s,N\}
.
In the same way, one can find
q_{0}\in[(2^{\ast}_{{}_{d}})^{\prime},d(d+2-N)^{-1})
such that

g\in L^{q_{0}}_{\mu}(\Gamma)\indent\indent\textrm{and}\indent\indent\frac{q_{0}}{Nq_{0}-2q_{0}-dq_{0}+d}=\frac{\tau_{r,s}}{d}.

Then, selecting
\vartheta_{p_{0},q_{0}}
as in (
3.61
), and applying multiple times Hölder’s inequality,
we end up getting the inequality

|\|\textbf{u}^{+}\||_{{}_{r,s}}\,\leq\,C_{2}\left(|\|(f,g)\||_{{}_{p_{0},q_{0}}}+\|u^{+}\|_{{}_{2,\Omega}}\right)\,\leq\,C^{\prime}_{2}\left(|\|(f,g)\||_{{}_{p,q}}+\|u^{+}\|_{{}_{2,\Omega}}\right),

(3.71)

for some constants
C_{2},\,C^{\prime}_{2}>0
. This establishes the fourth case.

\bullet

Case 5:

p>N/2
and
q>\frac{d}{d+2-N}
.

For this part, a complete proof is given for the parabolic case in the proof of Theorem
6
, so we will only sketch the main
steps of this corresponding elliptic version.
Let
u\in\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)
be a weak solution of problem (
1.5
), and
let
u_{k}
be the function given by (
3.30
). Then we have

\mathcal{E}_{\mu}(u,u_{k})=\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}}fu_{k}\,dx+\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma_{k}}gu_{k}\,d\mu,

(3.72)

where we recall the definition of the form
\mathcal{E}_{\mu}(\cdot,\cdot)
in (
3.1
). Estimating the right hand side in (
3.72
) and taking
into account Assumptions
(A3)
,
(B4)
, and (
1.4
), we apply Young’s inequality multiple times and use Remark
2
(d)
to infer (after multiple calculations) that

\mathcal{E}_{\mu}(u,u_{k})\,\geq\,\displaystyle\frac{c_{0}}{4}\|\nabla u_{k}\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega_{k}}}+c^{\#}\|u_{k}\|^{2}_{{}_{D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}})}}-\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}}\mathfrak{h}(|u_{k}|^{2}+k^{2})\,dx-\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma_{k}}\mathfrak{k}(|u_{k}|^{2}+k^{2})\,d\mu,

(3.73)

for some constant
c^{\#}>0
, where

\mathfrak{h}:=\frac{1}{c_{0}}\displaystyle\sum^{N}_{i=1}|\hat{a}_{i}|^{2}+M^{\star}+|\lambda|\in L^{{}^{\min\left\{\frac{r_{1}}{2},r_{3}\right\}}}(\Omega)^{+}

and

\mathfrak{k}:=\min\{1,c^{\star}_{1}\}(|c^{\ast}_{1}|+2|\beta|)+c^{\star}_{3}|\rho|\in L^{{}^{\min\{s,\theta\}}}_{\mu}(\Gamma)^{+},

for some constant
c^{\ast}_{1}>0
, and where

M^{\star}:=\max\left\{\|\hat{\mathfrak{a}}\|_{{}_{r_{1},\Omega}},\|\check{\mathfrak{a}}\|_{{}_{r_{2},\Omega}}\right\}\left(C_{1}\|\hat{\mathfrak{a}}\|_{{}_{r_{1},\Omega}}+C_{2}\|\check{\mathfrak{a}}\|_{{}_{r_{2},\Omega}}\right)/c_{0}>0,

for some constants
C_{1},\,C_{2}>0
. It is also easy to see that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}}fu_{k}\,dx+\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma_{k}}gu_{k}\,d\mu\,\leq\,\displaystyle\frac{1}{k}\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}}|f|(|u_{k}|^{2}+k^{2})\,dx+\displaystyle\frac{1}{k}\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma_{k}}|g|(|u_{k}|^{2}+k^{2})\,d\mu.

(3.74)

Substituting all this into (
3.72
) and recalling the equivalence of the norms
\|\cdot\|_{{}_{\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)}}

and
\|\cdot\|_{{}_{\mathcal{W}_{2}(\overline{\Omega})}}
, we deduce that

\min\left\{1,\displaystyle\frac{c_{0}}{4},c^{\#}\right\}\|u_{k}\|^{2}_{{}_{\mathcal{W}_{2}(\overline{\Omega})}}\,\leq\,c\|u_{k}\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega_{k}}}+c\left(\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}}\left(\frac{1}{k}|f|+\mathfrak{h}\right)(|u_{k}|^{2}+k^{2})\,dx+\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma_{k}}\left(\frac{1}{k}|g|+\mathfrak{k}\right)(|u_{k}|^{2}+k^{2})\,d\mu\right),

(3.75)

for some constant
c>0
. We now estimate the last two terms in (
3.75
). Put

\hat{k}^{2}_{0}:=\|f\|^{2}_{{}_{p,\Omega}}+\|g\|^{2}_{{}_{q,\Gamma}},\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{and}\,\,\,\,\,k\geq\hat{k}_{0},

(3.76)

and apply Hölder’s inequality together with Young’s inequality and Remark
2
(d) to deduce that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}}\frac{1}{k}|f||u_{k}|^{2}\,dx\,\leq\,\displaystyle\frac{1}{k}\|f\|_{{}_{p,\Omega}}\|u_{k}\|^{2}_{{}_{\frac{2p}{p-1},\Omega_{k}}}\,\leq\,\displaystyle\frac{k_{0}}{k}\left(\epsilon_{1}\|\nabla u_{k}\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega_{k}}}+C_{\epsilon_{1}}\|u_{k}\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega_{k}}}\right)\,\leq\,\epsilon_{1}\|u_{k}\|^{2}_{{}_{\mathcal{W}_{2}(\overline{\Omega})}}+C_{\epsilon_{1}}\|u_{k}\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega_{k}}},

(3.77)

for all
\epsilon_{1}>0
, and for some constant
C_{\epsilon_{1}}>0
. Also, since
\mathfrak{h}\in L^{{}^{\min\{r_{1},r_{3}\}}}(\Omega)
and

\mathfrak{r}:=2\min\{r_{1}/2,\,r_{3}\}\left(\min\{r_{1}/2,\,r_{3}\}-1\right)^{-1}<2N(N-2)^{-1},

proceeding in the same way as in the derivation of (
3.77
), we get that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}}\mathfrak{h}|u_{k}|^{2}\,dx\,\leq\,\|\mathfrak{h}\|_{{}_{\min\left\{\frac{r_{1}}{2},r_{3}\right\},\Omega}}\left(\epsilon_{2}\|u_{k}\|^{2}_{{}_{\mathcal{W}_{2}(\overline{\Omega})}}+C_{\epsilon_{2}}\|u_{k}\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega_{k}}}\right),

(3.78)

for every
\epsilon_{2}>0
, and for some constant
C_{\epsilon_{2}}>0
. Moreover, using the fact that
\mathfrak{k}\in L^{{}^{\min\{s,\theta\}}}_{\mu}(\Gamma)
with

\mathfrak{s}:=2\min\{s,\theta\}\left(\min\{s,\theta\}-1\right)^{-1}<2d(N-2)^{-1},

we proceed in the exact way as above to deduce that

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma_{k}}\frac{1}{k}|g||u_{k}|^{2}\,d\mu\,\leq\,\epsilon_{3}\|u_{k}\|^{2}_{{}_{\mathcal{W}_{2}(\overline{\Omega})}}+C_{\epsilon_{3}}\|u_{k}\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega_{k}}},

(3.79)

for all
\epsilon_{3}>0
, and for some constant
C_{\epsilon_{3}}>0
, and

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma_{k}}\mathfrak{k}|u_{k}|^{2}\,d\mu\,\leq\,\|\mathfrak{k}\|_{{}_{\min\{s,\theta\},\Gamma}}\left(\epsilon_{4}\|u_{k}\|^{2}_{{}_{\mathcal{W}_{2}(\overline{\Omega})}}+C_{\epsilon_{4}}\|u_{k}\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega_{k}}}\right),

(3.80)

for every
\epsilon_{4}>0
, and for some constant
C_{\epsilon_{4}}>0
. Choosing

\epsilon_{1}=\epsilon_{3}=\frac{1}{8}\min\left\{1,\displaystyle\frac{c_{0}}{4},c^{\#}\right\},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\epsilon_{2}=\frac{1}{8\|\mathfrak{h}\|_{{}_{\min\left\{\frac{r_{1}}{2},r_{3}\right\},\Omega}}}\min\left\{1,\displaystyle\frac{c_{0}}{4},c^{\#}\right\},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\epsilon_{4}=\frac{1}{8\|\mathfrak{k}\|_{{}_{\min\{s,\theta\},\Gamma}}}\min\left\{1,\displaystyle\frac{c_{0}}{4},c^{\#}\right\},

we insert (
3.77
), (
3.78
), (
3.79
), and (
3.80
) into (
3.75
), to arrive at

\|u_{k}\|^{2}_{{}_{\mathcal{W}_{2}(\overline{\Omega})}}\,\leq\,\displaystyle\frac{2c}{\min\{1,c_{0}/4,c^{\#}\}}\left\{C^{\star}_{0}\|u_{k}(t)\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega_{k}}}\right.+\left.k^{2}\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}}\left(\frac{1}{k}|f|+\mathfrak{h}\right)\,dx\,+\,k^{2}\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma_{k}}\left(\frac{1}{k}|g|+\mathfrak{k}\right)\,d\mu\right\},

(3.81)

where

C^{\star}_{0}:=1+C_{\epsilon_{1}}+C_{\epsilon_{2}}\|\mathfrak{h}\|_{{}_{\min\{r_{1},r_{3}\},\Omega}}+C_{\epsilon_{3}}+C_{\epsilon_{4}}\|\mathfrak{k}\|_{{}_{\min\{s,\theta\},\Gamma}}.

Having this, we now produce estimates in the last two integral terms in (
3.81
). In fact,
recalling that
k\geq k_{0}
and applying Hölder’s inequality, we see that

\int_{\Omega_{k}}\frac{1}{k}|f|\,dx\,\leq\,|\Omega_{k}|^{{}^{1-\frac{1}{p}}},\,\,\,\,\,\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}}\mathfrak{h}\,dx\,\leq\,\|\mathfrak{h}\|_{{}_{\min\left\{\frac{r_{1}}{2},r_{3}\right\},\Omega}}|\Omega_{k}|^{2/\mathfrak{r}},

(3.82)

and

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma_{k}}\frac{1}{k}|g|\,d\mu\,\leq\,\mu(\Gamma_{k})^{{}^{1-\frac{1}{q}}},\,\,\,\,\,\int_{\Gamma_{k}}\mathfrak{k}\,d\mu\,dt\,\leq\,\|\mathfrak{k}\|_{{}_{\min\{s,\theta\},\Gamma}}\mu(\Gamma_{k})^{2/\mathfrak{s}}dt.

(3.83)

Put

\gamma_{0}:=\displaystyle\frac{2c\max\left\{1,C^{\star}_{0},\|\mathfrak{h}\|_{{}_{\min\left\{\frac{r_{1}}{2},r_{3}\right\},\Omega}},\|\mathfrak{k}\|_{{}_{\min\{s,\theta\},\Gamma}}\right\}}{\min\{1,c_{0}/4,c^{\#}\}},

and we choose

\theta_{1,1}:=\frac{2p-N}{(N-2)p},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\theta_{1,2}:=\frac{2N+2\mathfrak{r}-N\mathfrak{r}}{(N-2)\mathfrak{r}},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,s_{1,1}=s_{1,2}:=2^{\ast}_{{}_{N}},

and

\theta_{2,1}:=\frac{dq+2q-Nq-d}{(N-2)q},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\theta_{2,2}:=\frac{2d+2\mathfrak{s}-N\mathfrak{s}}{(N-2)\mathfrak{s}},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,s_{2,1}=s_{2,2}:=2^{\ast}_{{}_{d}}.

Recalling the assumptions, one clearly sees that the parameters
\theta_{1,\xi},\,\theta_{2,\zeta}
are all positive.
Using this, we substitute (
3.82
), and (
3.83
) into (
3.81
), to arrive at

\|u_{k}\|^{2}_{{}_{\mathcal{W}_{2}(\overline{\Omega})}}\,\leq\,\gamma_{0}\left(\|u_{k}\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega_{k}}}+k^{2}\displaystyle\sum^{2}_{\xi=1}|\Omega_{k}|^{{}^{\frac{2(1+\theta_{1,\xi})}{s_{1,\xi}}}}+k^{2}\displaystyle\sum^{2}_{\zeta=1}\mu(\Gamma_{k})^{{}^{\frac{2(1+\theta_{2,\zeta})}{s_{2,\zeta}}}}\right).

(3.84)

Therefore, all the conditions of Lemma
3
are fulfilled, and thus
applying Lemma
3
to both
u
and
-u
(the latter one being a weak solution of (
1.5
) for
f,\,g
replaced by

-f,\,-g
, respectively) gives the desired
L^{\infty}
-bound, completing the proof of the theorem.

\Box

Remark 3
.

Under some additional conditions, the constants
m_{1}(p,q)
and
m_{2}(p,q)
in Theorem
2
can be improved.
Here we bring two particular situations. We are assuming that
N>2
.

(a)

If
p<N/2
and
q\geq p(N-2)(N-2p)^{-1}
, then it is easy to deduce (as in the proof of Theorem
2
)
that (
3.45
) holds for

m_{1}(p,q):=m_{1}(p)=\frac{Np}{N-2p}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,m_{2}(p,q):=m_{2}(p)=\frac{dp}{N-2p}.

(b)

Let
\Omega
be a bounded Lipschitz domain, and assume that condition
(C2)
holds. Then it is well-known that

\Gamma
is a compact
(N-1)
-dimensional Riemannian manifold, and thus (
2.7
) holds.
Consequently, given
p\geq(2^{\ast}_{{}_{N}})^{\prime}
and
q\geq(2^{\star}_{{}_{\Gamma}})^{\prime}:=2(N-1)(N+1)^{-1}
,
if
N>3
, the conclusions of Theorem
2
hold in this case for

m_{1}(p,q):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\frac{N}{N-2}\min\left\{\frac{p(N-2)}{N-2p},\,\frac{q(N-3)}{N-2q-1}\right\},\,\,\textrm{if}\,\,p<N/2\,\,\textrm{and}\,\,q<\frac{N-1}{2},\\[4.30554pt]
\frac{Np}{Np-2p},\,\,\textrm{if}\,\,p<N/2\,\,\textrm{and}\,\,\frac{N-1}{2}\leq q,\\[4.30554pt]
\frac{(N-1)q}{N-2q-1},\,\,\textrm{if}\,\,q<\frac{N-1}{2}\,\,\textrm{and}\,\,p\geq N/2,\\[4.30554pt]
r\in(2^{\ast}_{{}_{N}},\infty),\,\,\textrm{if}\,\,p=N/2\,\,\textrm{and}\,\,q\geq\frac{N-1}{2},\,\,\textrm{or}\,\,q=\frac{N-1}{2}\,\,\textrm{and}\,\,p\geq N/2,\\[4.30554pt]
\,\,\,\,\,\infty,\,\,\textrm{if}\,\,p>N/2\,\,\,\textrm{and}\,\,\,q>\frac{N-1}{2},\end{array}\right.

and

m_{2}(p,q):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\frac{N-1}{N-2q-1}\min\left\{\frac{p(N-2)}{N-2p},\,\frac{q(N-3)}{N-2q-1}\right\},\,\,\textrm{if}\,\,p<N/2\,\,\textrm{and}\,\,q<\frac{N-1}{2},\\[4.30554pt]
\frac{Np}{Np-2p},\,\,\textrm{if}\,\,p<N/2\,\,\textrm{and}\,\,\frac{N-1}{2}\leq q,\\[4.30554pt]
\frac{(N-1)q}{N-2q-1},\,\,\textrm{if}\,\,q<\frac{N-1}{2}\,\,\textrm{and}\,\,p\geq N/2,\\[4.30554pt]
s\in(2^{\star}_{{}_{\Gamma}},\infty),\,\,\textrm{if}\,\,p=N/2\,\,\textrm{and}\,\,q\geq\frac{N-1}{2},\,\,\textrm{or}\,\,q=\frac{N-1}{2}\,\,\textrm{and}\,\,p\geq N/2,\\[4.30554pt]
\,\,\,\,\,\infty,\,\,\textrm{if}\,\,p>N/2\,\,\,\textrm{and}\,\,\,q>\frac{N-1}{2},\end{array}\right.

Furthermore, when
N=3
, we obtain that

m_{1}(p,q)=m_{p}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\frac{Np}{N-2p},\,\,\textrm{if}\,\,p<N/2,\\[4.30554pt]
r\in(2^{\ast}_{{}_{N}},\infty),\,\,\textrm{if}\,\,p=N/2,\\[4.30554pt]
\,\,\,\,\,\infty,\,\,\textrm{if}\,\,p>N/2,\end{array}\right.

and

m_{2}(p,q)=m_{q}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}s\in(2^{\star}_{{}_{\Gamma}},\infty),\,\,\textrm{if}\,\,p\leq N/2,\\[4.30554pt]
\,\,\,\,\,\infty,\,\,\textrm{if}\,\,p>N/2.\end{array}\right.

These improvements hold in the case when
\Gamma
is a compact
(N-1)
-dimensional Riemannian manifold,
and
\mu=\mu(\mathfrak{g})
(for
\mathfrak{g}
the Riemannian metric on
\Gamma
), provided that all the conditions in Assumption
1

are met, with the exception that in this case it is not required that the upper Alhfors condition for the measure
\mu(\mathfrak{g})
.
More details about this are given in subsection
6.1
.

Remark 4
.

We consider the situation when the form
\mathcal{E}_{\mu}(\cdot,\cdot)
is coercive. In this case the right hand bound in (
3.45
) becomes independent on
u
, as we show below.

(a)

Let
\xi\in\mathbb{R\!}
, and assume that
the measurable coefficient
\lambda(x)
in the differential operator
\mathcal{A}
in (
1.1
) is of the form

\lambda(x):=\upsilon(x)+\xi
, where
\upsilon\in L^{r_{3}}(\Omega)
(
r_{3}>N/2
) and
\xi>\delta^{\star}_{0}
, for

\delta^{\star}_{0}>0
denotes the constant given by (
3.24
) for
\lambda
replaced by
\upsilon
(in (
3.24
)). Then one can deduce that the
bilinear form
\mathcal{E}_{\mu}
is coercive, and consequently, by virtue of Theorem
2
, if
u\in\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)
solves (
1.5
), we have that

|\|\mathbf{u}\||_{{}_{m_{1}(p,q),m_{2}(p,q)}}\,\leq\,c\,|\|(f,g)\||_{{}_{p,q}},

(3.85)

for some constant
c>0
, where the constants
m_{1}(p,q),\,m_{2}(p,q)
are given by (
3.46
) and (
3.47
),
respectively. To see this, observe that if

u\in\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)
solves (
3.26
), then
u
is a weak solution of the boundary value problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{A}u\,=\,f-\xi u\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{in}\,\,\Omega\\
\mathcal{B}u\,=\,\,\,\,\,g\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{on}\,\,\Gamma\\
\end{array}\right.

(3.86)

where the differential operators
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are given in (
1.1
) and (
1.2
),
respectively. Hence, following the proof of Proposition
1
, we notice that

\|u\|^{2}_{{}_{\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)}}\,\leq\,\displaystyle\frac{2}{\min\{c_{0},c^{\star}_{0}\}}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\mu}(u,u)+\delta^{\star}_{0}\|u\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega}}\right)

=\displaystyle\frac{2}{\min\{c_{0},c^{\star}_{0}\}}\left(\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}fu\,dx+\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}gu\,d\mu-\left(\xi-\delta^{\star}_{0})\right)\|u\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega}}\right)\,\leq\,\displaystyle\frac{2c^{\prime}}{\min\{c_{0},c^{\star}_{0}\}}|\|(f,g)\||_{{}_{p,q}}\|u\|_{{}_{\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)}},

for some constant
c^{\prime}>0
. This proves our claim.

(b)

For bounded extension domains whose boundaries are upper
d
-sets (with respect to
\mu
),
for
d\in(N-2,N)
, one has that Remark
2
(b)
holds. Then, consider now the case when
\beta(x)=\beta_{0}(x)+\zeta
, for
\beta_{0}\in L^{s}_{\mu}(\Gamma)
(
s>d(d+2-N)^{-1}
)
and
\zeta>\delta^{\star}_{0}C_{\tau^{\ast}}
, where
\delta^{\star}_{0}>0
is given by (
3.24
) (for
\beta_{0}
instead of
\beta
), and for

\tau^{\ast}:=\frac{\min\{c_{0},\,c^{\star}_{0}\}}{4\delta^{\star}_{0}}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,C_{\tau^{\ast}}>0\,\,\,\textrm{is a constant}.

Given
u\in\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)
a weak solution of (
1.5
),
u
solves the boundary value problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{A}u\,=\,\,\,\,\,f\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{in}\,\,\Omega\\
\mathcal{B}u\,=\,g-\zeta u\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{on}\,\,\Gamma\\
\end{array}\right.

(3.87)

Following as above together with the compactness of the embedding
\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)\hookrightarrow L^{2}_{\mu}(\Gamma)
and
Remark
2
(d), we see that

\|u\|^{2}_{{}_{\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)}}\,\leq\,\displaystyle\frac{2}{\min\{c_{0},c^{\star}_{0}\}}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\mu}(u,u)+\delta^{\star}_{0}\|u\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega}}\right)

\indent\indent\leq\,\displaystyle\frac{2}{\min\{c_{0},c^{\star}_{0}\}}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\mu}(u,u)+\delta^{\star}_{0}\left\{\tau^{\ast}\|\nabla u\|_{{}_{2,\Omega}}+C_{\tau^{\ast}}\|u\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Gamma}}\right\}\right).

Then, as in Case (a), using the above estimate, we get that

\|u\|^{2}_{{}_{\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)}}\,\leq\,\displaystyle\frac{4}{\min\{c_{0},c^{\star}_{0}\}}\left(\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}fu\,dx+\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}gu\,d\mu-\left(\zeta-\delta^{\star}_{0}C_{\tau^{\ast}})\right)\|u\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Gamma}}\right)\,\leq\,\displaystyle\frac{4c^{\prime\prime}}{\min\{c_{0},c^{\star}_{0}\}}|\|(f,g)\||_{{}_{p,q}}\|u\|_{{}_{\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)}},

for some constant
c^{\prime\prime}>0
, and thus (
3.85
) holds as well for this case.

(c)

If
\xi\geq\delta^{\star}_{0}
in part (a), or if
\zeta\geq\delta^{\star}_{0}C_{\tau^{\ast}}
in part (b), then by replacing the space

H^{1}(\Omega)
by the space
H_{0}(\Omega)
in the case condition
(C1)
holds, then one gets the coercivity of the form

\mathcal{E}_{\mu}(\cdot,\cdot)
, and in particular, this shows that under these conditions, problem (
1.4
) possesses a unique
(modulo constant) weak solution
u\in H^{1}(\Omega)
. Same conclusion happens in the case when condition
(C2)
is fulfilled, by replacing the
set
H^{1}_{\mu}(\Omega;\Gamma)
by the closed subspace
H_{0,\mu}(\Omega;\Gamma)
.

We conclude this section by stating our second main assumption.

Assumption 2
.

Let the conditions
(A1)
,
(A2)
,
(A3)
,
(B1)
,
(B2)
,
(B3)
, and
(B4)
hold.

3.3.
Inverse positivity

In this part we present an inverse positivity property and dependence result for
for weak solutions of the boundary value problem (
1.5
).

Given the operators
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
defined by (
1.1
) and (
1.2
),
we are concerned with solutions for the differential inequality

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{A}u\,\geq\,0\,\,\,\indent\textrm{in}\,\,\Omega\\
\mathcal{B}u\,\geq\,0\,\,\,\indent\textrm{on}\,\,\Gamma\\
\end{array}\right.

(3.88)

The above inequalities can be understood in the weak sense, that is, we consider solutions of the inequality

\mathcal{E}_{\mu}(u,\varphi)\,\geq\,0,\indent\,\,\forall\,\varphi\in\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)^{+},

(3.89)

where the form
\mathcal{E}_{\mu}
is given by (
3.1
). The following is the main result of this subsection.

Theorem 3
.

Suppose that all the conditions of Assumption
1
are fulfilled, suppose that
conditions
(A2)
and
(B3)
hold, and assume that any of the cases in Remark
4
occur.
If
u\in\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)
is a solution of (
3.88
), then

\mathbf{u}\geq\mathbf{0}
; that is,
u\geq 0
a.e. in
\Omega
, and
u|_{{}_{\Gamma}}\geq 0

\mu
-a.e. in
\Gamma
.

Proof.

Let
u\in\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)
be a solution of (
3.88
), and assume that Remark
4
(a)
holds (the other case follows in a similar way). By
(A2)
and
(B3)
we have

K(u^{+},u^{-})\leq 0
, and
\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}}(u^{+},u^{-})\leq 0
, and consequently
\mathcal{E}_{\mu}(u^{+},u^{-})\leq 0
. Write

\mathfrak{C}^{\star}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\xi-\delta^{\star}_{0},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{if Remark \ref{coercivity}(a) (or Remark \ref{coercivity}(c) related to (a)) holds};\\
\zeta-\delta^{\star}_{0}C_{\tau^{\ast}},\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{if Remark \ref{coercivity}(b) (or Remark \ref{coercivity}(c) related to (b)) holds};\\
\end{array}\right.

Then
\mathfrak{C}^{\star}>0
. Since
u\in\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)
satisfies the inequality
(
3.89
), proceeding as in Proposition
1
and Remark
4
, we obtain that

|\|\mathbf{u}^{-}\||^{2}_{{}_{2}}\,\leq\,c\left(\mathcal{E}_{\mu}(u^{-},u^{-})-\mathfrak{C}^{\star}\|u^{-}\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega}}\right)\,\leq\,-c\,\mathcal{E}_{\mu}(u,u^{-})\,\leq 0,

(3.90)

for some constant
c>0
. Thus
\mathbf{u}^{-}=\mathbf{0}
, which shows that
\mathbf{u}\geq\mathbf{0}
a.e., as desired.

\Box

From here one has the following immediate consequence.

Corollary 1
.

Under the assumptions in Theorem
3
, let
u\in\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)
be a weak solution of problem (
1.5
).
If
f\geq 0
a.e. in
\Omega
and
g\geq 0

\mu
-a.e. on
\Gamma
, then
\mathbf{u}\geq\mathbf{0}
.

4.
The parabolic problem

In this section we turn our attention to the solvability and regularity theory for the parabolic problem (
1.8
),
under the conditions in Assumption
1
. Also, as before, we will concentrate in the case
N>2
.

4.1.
The homogeneous problem

We begin by discussing the solvability of the time-dependent problem (
1.8
) for
f=g=0
. To begin, recall the bilinear form

\mathcal{E}_{\mu}(\cdot,\cdot)
given by (
3.1
).
Since the form
\mathcal{E}_{\mu}
is closed in
L^{2}(\Omega),
there exists a unique operator
A_{\mu}

with domain
D(A_{\mu})\subseteq\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)
such that
D(A_{\mu})
is dense in
L^{2}(\Omega)
, and

A_{\mu}
is the generator of the form
\mathcal{E}_{\mu}(\cdot,\cdot)
in the sense that

\mathcal{E}_{\mu}(u,v)=\langle A_{\mu}u,v\rangle_{{}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{for each}\,\,u\in D(A_{\mu})\,\,\,\textrm{and for all}\,\,v\in\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma).

Now let
\{T_{\mu}(t)\}_{t\geq 0}
denote the
C_{0}
-semigroup generated by
-A_{\mu}
in
L^{2}(\Omega)
. By the theory of forms, in views of Proposition

1
, it follows that
\{T_{\mu}(t)\}_{t\geq 0}
is analytic and compact (Cf.
[
36
, Chapter 17, Section 6]
).
Consequently, for each
u_{0}\in L^{2}(\Omega)
, the function
u(t):=T_{\mu}(t)u_{0}
is the unique mild solution of the Cauchy problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}u_{t}=A_{\mu}u\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{for}\,\,t\in(0,\infty);\\
u(0)=u_{0}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{in}\,\,\Omega\end{array}\right.

(4.1)

(e.g. Definition
7
(b)), and problem (
4.1
) is precisely the abstract formulation of problem (
1.8
) for
f=g=0
.

To proceed, we first make an assumption over the Wentzell-type space
D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}})
, which is the following:

(B5)

For every bounded function
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R\!}^{N})
with bounded gradient, one has

e^{\varphi}u\in D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}})
for each
u\in D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}})
.

Then, given
\varphi
as in
(B5)
, one clearly have that
e^{\varphi}u\in\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)
whenever
u\in\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)
.

Continuing with the discussion, by recalling (
2.5
) and condition

(B5)
, one proceeds as in
[
84
, proof of Theorem 6.8]
to get the existence of a constant
c>0
such that

\|T_{\mu}(t)\|_{{}_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(\Omega);L^{\infty}(\Omega))}}\,\leq\,ct^{-N/4}e^{-\delta_{0}t},\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{for all}\,\,t>0,

(4.2)

where
\delta_{0}>
is a constant depending on the constant
\delta^{\star}_{0}
given by (
3.24
). In particular, the above inequality
shows that the semigroup
\{T_{\mu}(t)\}_{t\geq 0}
is ultracontractive, that is,
T_{\mu}(t)
maps
L^{2}(\Omega)
into
L^{\infty}(\Omega)
.
In addition, by working with the adjoint semigroup
\{T^{\ast}_{\mu}(t)\}_{t\geq 0}
and performing a duality argument,
we can give a corresponding
L^{1}-L^{2}
estimate norm for
\{T_{\mu}(t)\}_{t\geq 0}
in the sense that

\|T_{\mu}(t)\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{1}(\Omega);L^{\infty}(\Omega))}\leq ct^{-N/2}e^{-\delta_{0}t},\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{for each}\,\,t>0.

Therefore, by Dunford Pettis’s Theorem, the semigroup
\{T_{\mu}(t)\}_{t\geq 0}

admits an integral representation with kernel
K_{\mu}(x,y,t)\in L^{\infty}(\Omega\times\Omega)
(e.g.
[
12
, Theorem 1.3]
).
By proceeding as in
[
33
, Theorem 6.1]
, taking into account
(B5)
, and using perturbation techniques due to Davies
[
37
]
,
we can establish the existence of a constant
c>0
such that

|K_{\mu}(t,x,y)|\,\leq\,ct^{-N/2}e^{\epsilon t+\delta_{0}\sigma t}e^{-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4\delta_{0}t}},

for all
\epsilon>0
, where
\sigma>0
denotes a constant depending on the coefficients in the definition of the constant
\delta^{\star}_{0}
in (
3.24
)
(Cf.
[
84
, Theorem 6.10]
).

Finally, by the above inequality the kernel
K_{\mu}
satisfies Gaussian estimates, and consequently in views of
[
16
]
,
it follows that
\{T_{\mu}(t)\}_{t\geq 0}
extrapolates
to a family of compact analytic semigroups over
L^{p}(\Omega)
for all
p\in[1,\infty]
, which are strongly continuous whenever
p\in[1,\infty)
,
and all have the same angle of analyticity. Furthermore, one has that
\{T_{\mu}(z)\}_{{}_{\textrm{Re}(z)>0}}
are kernel operators satisfying Gaussian estimates.
In particular, for each
u_{0}\in L^{p}(\Omega)
with
1\leq p<\infty
, the function
u(t):=T_{\mu}(t)u_{0}\in L^{p}(\Omega)
is the unique mild solution
to problem (
4.1
) (and consequently the homogeneous parabolic problem (
1.8
) in
L^{p}(\Omega)
).
Moreover, following the approach as in
[
33
, Proposition 7.1]
, we find the existence of constants

M\geq 0
and
\omega\in\mathbb{R\!}\,
(depending on
N
,
|\Omega|
, and the coefficients in the definition of the constant
\delta^{\ast}
in (
3.54
)),
fulfilling

\|u(t)\|_{{}_{\infty,\Omega}}\,\leq\,Me^{\omega t}\|u_{0}\|_{{}_{\infty,\Omega}}

(4.3)

for each
u_{0}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
.

4.2.
Solvability of the inhomogeneous problem

We now turn our attention to the realization of the inhomogeneous boundary value problem (
1.8
). Due to the fact that one is considering inhomogeneous
boundary conditions, the method relying in semigroup theory is not applicable anymore, so we will proceed with an analogous procedure as in
[
80
]
,
with some modifications in order to incorporate nonlocal operators and Wentzell-type maps.

We start right away by introducing the notion of weak solutions to problem (
1.8
).

Definition 5
.

Let
T>0
be a fixed real number. Then we say that a function
u\in C([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}((0,T),\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma))
is a
weak solution of the
parabolic equation (
1.8
) on
[0,T]
, if

-\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}u(\xi)\psi_{t}(\xi)\,dxd\xi+\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{E}_{\mu}(u(\xi),\psi(\xi))\,d\xi

=\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}u_{0}\psi(0)dx+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}f(\xi,x)\psi(\xi)\,dxd\xi+\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma}g(\xi,x)\psi(\xi)d\mu d\xi,

(4.4)

for all
\psi\in H^{1}(0,T;\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma))
such that
\psi(T)=0
, where
u_{0}:=u(0)
and where
\mathcal{E}_{\mu}(\cdot,\cdot)

denotes the bilinear form given by (
3.1
).
Similarly, we say that a function
u:[0,\infty)\to L^{2}(\Omega)
is a weak solution of (
1.8
) on
[0,\infty)
if for every
T>0

its restriction to
[0,T]
is a weak solution on
[0,T].

We now define an operator
\mathbf{A}
acting on
\mathbb{X\!}^{\,2}(\Omega,\Gamma)
, which is directly related with the notion of weak solutions
of problem (
1.5
), and will allow to transition from the inhomogeneous elliptic problem to the inhomogeneous parabolic problem.
As commented by Nittka
[
80
]
, this operator
\mathbf{A}
is not densely defined and hence it is not the
generator of a strongly continuous semigroup. In fact, it turns out that
\mathbf{A}
does not even satisfy the Hille-Yosida estimates.
However, one will show that the operator is resolvent positive and
hence generates a once integrated semigroup, which will imply existence and uniqueness of solutions for regular data and gives information about the
asymptotic behavior of solutions.

Definition 6
.

Given
u\in\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)
, let
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
be the linear operators defined by (
1.1
) and (
1.2
), respectively,
and define

\mathcal{A}_{0}=\mathcal{A}-\mathcal{J}_{{}_{\Omega}}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\mathcal{N}^{\ast}_{\mu}u:=\displaystyle\sum^{N}_{{}^{i,j=1}}(\alpha_{ij}(x)\partial_{x_{j}}u+\hat{a}_{j}(x)u)\nu_{\mu_{i}}.

(a)

We say that
\mathcal{A}u\in L^{2}(\Omega)
if there exists a
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
such that

\mathcal{E}_{{}_{\Omega}}(u,v)+(\mathcal{J}_{{}_{\Omega}}u)v=-\langle f,v\rangle_{2},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{for all}\,\,v\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega),

where
\mathcal{E}_{{}_{\Omega}}(\cdot,\cdot)
is defined by (
3.3
), and for
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{2}
denoting the inner product on
L^{2}(\Omega)
.

(b)

Assume that
\mathcal{A}_{0}u\in L^{2}(\Omega)
. Then we say that
\mathcal{N}^{\ast}_{\mu}u\in L^{2}_{\mu}(\Gamma)
,
if there exists a unique function
g\in L^{2}_{\mu}(\Gamma)
such that

\langle-\mathcal{A}_{0}u,v\rangle_{2}+\langle g,v\rangle_{{}_{2,\Gamma}}=\mathcal{E}_{{}_{\Omega}}(u,v),\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{for every}\,\,v\in H^{1}(\Omega),

where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{{}_{2,\Gamma}}
denotes the inner product over
L^{2}_{\mu}(\Gamma)
.

(c)

We say that
\mathcal{B}_{0}u:=\left(\mathcal{B}-\mathcal{N}^{\ast}_{\mu}\right)u\in L^{2}_{\mu}(\Gamma)
if there exists a
g^{\prime}\in L^{2}_{\mu}(\Gamma)
such that

\mathcal{E}_{\mu}(u,v)=-\langle g^{\prime},v\rangle_{{}_{2,\Gamma}},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{for all}\,\,v\in\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma),

where
\mathcal{E}_{\mu}(\cdot,\cdot)
is defined by (
3.1
).

(d)

We define the operator
\mathbf{A}:\mathbb{X\!}^{\,2}(\Omega,\Gamma)\rightarrow\mathbb{X\!}^{\,2}(\Omega,\Gamma)
by:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}D(\mathbf{A}):=\left\{(u,0)\mid u\in\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma),\,\,\,\mathcal{A}u\in L^{2}(\Omega),\,\,\,\mathcal{B}u\in L^{2}_{\mu}(\Gamma)\right\};\\[4.30554pt]
\mathbf{A}(u,0):=(\mathcal{A}u,-\mathcal{B}u)=\left(\mathcal{A}u,\,-\mathcal{N}^{\ast}_{\mu}u-\mathcal{L}_{\mu}u-\beta u-\Theta_{{}_{\Gamma}}u\right).\end{array}\right.

Remark 5
.

In views of Definition
6
(d), it is easily verified that
(u,0)\in D(\mathbf{A})
with
-\mathbf{A}(u,0)=(f,g)
if and only if

\mathcal{E}_{\mu}(u,v)=\langle f,v\rangle_{2}+\langle g,v\rangle_{{}_{2,\Gamma}}

for every
v\in\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)
. In particular if
(f,g)\in\mathbb{X\!}^{\,2}(\Omega,\Gamma)
, then

(u,0)\in D(\mathbf{A})
with
-\mathbf{A}(u,0)=(f,g)
if and only if
u\in\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)
is a weak solution of (
1.5
).

For the rest of the section, we will assume that
\mathcal{L}_{\mu}\neq 0
(otherwise the proofs of the results become simpler).
We begin by establishing some basic properties for the operator
\mathbf{A}
.

Proposition 2
.

The operator
\mathbf{A}
is resolvent positive. More precisely, the operator

\lambda-\mathbf{A}
on
D(\mathbf{A})
is invertible for all
\gamma>\mathfrak{C}^{\star}_{0}
, where

\mathfrak{C}^{\star}_{0}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\delta^{\star}_{0},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{if Remark \ref{coercivity}(a) holds},\\
\delta^{\star}_{0}C_{\tau^{\ast}},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{if Remark \ref{coercivity}(b) holds}\end{array}\right.

(where we recall the definition of
\delta^{\star}_{0}>0
given in (
3.24
)),
and if
\mathbf{A}(u,0)=(f,g)
for
(f,g)\in\mathbb{X\!}^{\,2}(\Omega,\Gamma)
are such that

f\geq 0
and
g\geq 0
, then
u\geq 0
a.e. Moreover,
if
D(\mathbf{A})
is equipped with the graph norm, then
D(\mathbf{A})
is continuously embedded into
\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)\times\{0\}.

Proof.

If
\gamma>\mathfrak{C}^{\ast}_{0}
, then by virtue of Remark
4
one sees that (
3.25
) holds (for
\gamma^{\ast}=\gamma>0
),
and consequently by Proposition
1
together with Lax-Milgram’s Lemma,
for each
(f,g)\in\mathbb{X\!}^{\,2}(\Omega,\Gamma)
, there exists a unique function
u\in{\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)}
fulfilling

\gamma\int_{\Omega}uv\,dx+\mathcal{E}_{\mu}(u,v)=\langle f,v\rangle_{2}+\langle g,v\rangle_{{}_{2,\Gamma}},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{for all}\,\,v\in\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma).

This together with Remark
5
we have that
(\gamma-\mathbf{A})(u,0)=(\gamma u,0)-\mathbf{A}(u,0)=(f,g)
, which implies
that the operator
\gamma-\mathbf{A}:D(\mathbf{A})\rightarrow\mathbb{X\!}^{\,2}(\Omega,\Gamma)
is a bijection for each

\gamma>\mathfrak{C}^{\ast}_{0}
. Assume now
f\geq 0
and
g\geq 0.
Then this means that

\langle f,v\rangle_{2}\geq 0
for each
v\in H^{1}(\Omega)^{+}
, and

\langle g,w|_{{}_{\Gamma}}\rangle_{{}_{2,\Gamma}}\geq 0
for all
w\in H^{1}(\Omega)^{+}.

Letting
(u,0):=(\gamma-\mathbf{A})^{-1}(f,g)
and proceeding in the exact way as in the proof of Theorem
3
, we get that

\|u^{-}\|^{2}_{{}_{2^{\ast}_{{}_{N}},\Omega}}\,\leq\,c\left(\gamma\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}(u^{-})^{2}\,dx+\mathcal{E}_{\mu}(u^{-},u^{-})\right)\,\leq\,-c\left(\langle f,u^{-}\rangle_{\mathfrak{s}/2}+\langle g,u^{-}\rangle_{{}_{D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}})^{\ast},D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}})}}\right)\,\leq\,0,

for some constant
c>0
.
Therefore
u^{-}=0
a.e. implying that
u\geq 0
a.e., as asserted. Hence the resolvent
(\gamma-\mathbf{A})^{-1}
is a positive operator.
Since every positive operator is continuous we deduce that
\gamma-\mathbf{A}
is invertible. Moreover, since the operator
\mathbf{A}
is clearly closed,
we have that
D(\mathbf{A})
is a Banach space with respect to the graph norm. Since the embedding
D(\mathbf{A})\hookrightarrow\mathbb{X\!}^{\,2}(\Omega,\Gamma)

and the embedding
\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)\times\{0\}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{X\!}^{\,2}(\Omega,\Gamma)
are both bounded, with

D(\mathbf{A})\subseteq\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)\times\{0\}
, an application of the Closed Graph Theorem implies that the embedding

D(\mathbf{A})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)\times\{0\}
is continuous, completing the proof.

\Box

We now introduce the notion of mild and classical solution of (
1.8
), according to
[
74
]
.
Form now on we assume that
f\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))
and
g\in L^{2}(0,T,L^{2}_{\mu}(\Gamma))
.

Definition 7
.

Let
I=[0,T]
or
I=[0,\infty]
.

(a)

We say that function
u
is a
classical solution of problem (
1.8
)
, if

u\in C^{1}((0,T],L^{2}(\Omega))
with
u(0)=u_{0},
the mapping
t\to(u(t),0)
lies in
C(I,D(\mathbf{A}))
, and

(u_{t}(\xi),0)-\mathbf{A}(u(\xi),0)=(f(\xi,x),g(\xi,x)),\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{for all}\,\,\xi\in I.

(b)

We call
u
is a
mild solution of problem (
1.8
)
,
if
u\in C(I,L^{2}(\Omega)))
with
(\int_{0}^{t}u(\xi)\,d\xi,0)\in D(\mathbf{A})
for all
t\geq 0
, and

(u(t)-u_{0},0)-\mathbf{A}(\int_{0}^{t}u(\xi)\,d\xi,0)=(\int_{0}^{t}f(\xi,x)\,d\xi,\int_{0}^{t}g(\xi,x)\,d\xi)
.

The next two results can be established in the exact way as in
[
80
, proof of Theorem 2.6 and Proposition 2.7]
, and thus
their proofs will be omitted.

Theorem 4
.

Let
I=[0,T]
or
I=[0,\infty).
Then every classical solution of problem (
1.8
) on
I
is a weak solution of problem (
1.8
) on
I
, and
every weak solution of equation (
1.8
) on
I
is a mild solution of the heat equation (
1.8
) on
I.

Proposition 3
.

Let
u_{0}\in L^{2}(\Omega)
,
f\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))
and
g\in L^{2}(0,T,L^{2}_{\mu}(\Gamma)),
for some
T>0
.
Then problem (
1.8
) has a unique mild solution. Furthermore, if
(u_{0},0)\in D(\mathbf{A}),

\mathbf{A}(u_{0},0)+(f(0,x),g(0,x))\in D(\mathbf{A})
,

f\in C^{2}([0,T],L^{2}(\Omega))
, and
g\in C^{2}([0,T],L^{2}_{\mu}(\Gamma))
, then equation (
1.8
) has a classical solution.
Moreover, if
u_{0}\geq 0
,
f(t,x)\geq 0
, and
g(t,x)\geq 0
almost everywhere for each
t\in(0,T)
, then if
u
is a mild solution of
problem (
1.8
), then
u(t)\geq 0
almost everywhere for every
t\in(0,T)
.

In order to prove that there exists a unique weak solution for given data
u_{0},\,f,\,g
square
summable we need to prove some a priori bounds for the classical
L^{2}
-solutions.

Proposition 4
.

Let
u
be a classical solution of (
1.8
) on
[0,T],
for some
T>0
. Then

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u(t)|^{2}dx+\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\|\nabla u(\xi)\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega}}\,d\xi+\displaystyle\int^{T}_{0}\|u(\xi)\|^{2}_{{}_{D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}})}}\,d\xi

\leq\,c\left(\|u_{0}\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega}}+\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\|f(\xi)\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega}}d\xi+\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\|g(\xi)\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Gamma}}d\xi\right),

(4.5)

where
c
is a positive constant depending on
T,\,|\Omega|,\,\gamma,\,\bar{\alpha}.

Proof.

The proof runs in a similar manner as in
[
80
, proof of Lemma 2.9]
. However, since there are some modifications, we provide a complete proof.
Given
t\in[0,T]
, we apply Young’s inequality together with (
2.6
) and (
3.25
) to calculate that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u(t)|^{2}\,dx-\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u_{0}|^{2}\,dx\right)=\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}u(\xi)u_{t}(\xi)\,dxd\xi=

=\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\langle\mathcal{A}u(\xi),u(\xi)\rangle_{\mathfrak{s}/2}\,d\xi+\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}f(\xi,x)u(\xi)\,dxd\xi=\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\langle\mathcal{N}^{\ast}_{\mu}u(\xi),u(\xi)\rangle_{r_{d}/2}\,d\xi+

\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,+\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}f(\xi,x)u(\xi)\,dxd\xi-\int_{0}^{t}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{{}_{\Omega}}(u(\xi),u(\xi))+\left(\mathcal{J}_{{}_{\Omega}}u(\xi)\right)u(\xi)\right\}\,d\xi

=\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}f(\xi,x)u(\xi)\,dxd\xi+\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}g(\xi,x)u(\xi)\,d\mu d\xi-\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{E}_{\mu}(u(\xi),u(\xi))\,d\xi

\leq\,\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\|f(\xi)\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega}}\,d\xi+\displaystyle\frac{1}{4\epsilon}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\|g(\xi)\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Gamma}}\,d\xi

-(\eta-\epsilon c_{2}^{2})\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\left\{\|\nabla u(\xi)\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega}}+\|u(\xi)\|^{2}_{{}_{D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}})}}\right\}d\xi+\left(\gamma+\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon c_{2}^{2}\right)\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\|u(\xi)\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega}}\,d\xi.

By choosing
\epsilon:=\eta/2c_{2}^{2}
, and letting
c^{\prime}:=2\min\{\eta,1\}^{-1}>0
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\sup_{\xi\in[0,t]}\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u(\xi)|^{2}dx+\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla u(\xi)\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega}}\,d\xi+\displaystyle\int^{t}_{0}\|u(\xi)\|^{2}_{{}_{D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}})}}\,d\xi

\leq\,\displaystyle\frac{c^{\prime}}{2}\left(\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u_{0}|^{2}dx+\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|f(\xi)|^{2}\,dxd\xi+\displaystyle\frac{c^{2}_{2}}{\eta}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Gamma}|g(\xi)|^{2}\,dxd\xi\,+\,t(2\gamma+1+\eta)\displaystyle\sup_{\xi\in[0,t]}\int_{\Omega}|u(\xi)|^{2}\,dx\right).

Since the above inequality holds for all
t\in[0,T]
, by choosing
t^{\ast}:=(2c^{\prime}\gamma+c^{\prime}+c^{\prime}\eta)^{-1}>0
and putting
c^{\prime\prime}:=c^{\prime}\max\{1,c^{2}_{2}\eta^{-1}\}>0
,
we obtain that

\displaystyle\sup_{\xi\in[0,t]}\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u(\xi)|^{2}dx+\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla u(\xi)\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega}}\,d\xi+\displaystyle\int^{t}_{0}\|u(\xi)\|^{2}_{{}_{D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}})}}\,d\xi\,\leq\,c^{\prime\prime}\left(\|u_{0}\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega}}+\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\|f(\xi)\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega}}d\xi+\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\|g(\xi)\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Gamma}}d\xi\right),

for all
t\in[0,t^{\ast}]
. To complete the proof, given
T>0
arbitrary fixed, we can write
[0,T]:=\displaystyle\bigcup^{m}_{j=1}[y_{j-1},y_{j}]
for some
m\in\mathbb{N\!}\,
,
where
\displaystyle\max_{1\leq j\leq m}\{y_{j}-y_{j-1}\}\leq t^{\ast}
,
y_{0}=0
, and
y_{m}=T
. Applying the previous inequality in each of the subintervals

[y_{j-1},y_{j}]
, we are lead into the fulfillment of (
4.5
), as desired.

\Box

Remark 6
.

By suitable approximation by classical solutions and Proposition
3
(e.g.
[
80
, proof of Theorem 2.11]
)
we can deduce that Proposition
4
is valid for weak solutions
u\in C(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(0,T;\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma))

of problem (
1.8
).

From here, we are ready to establish the existence and uniqueness of a weak solution for problem (
1.8
). Again their proofs mimic
the procedures in
[
80
, proof of Theorem 2.11]
, and consequently they will be omitted.

Theorem 5
.

Given
T>0
arbitrary fixed, let
u_{0}\in L^{2}(\Omega)
,
f\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))
and
g\in L^{2}(0,T,L^{2}_{\mu}(\Gamma))
.
Then there exists a weak solution of (
1.8
) in
[0,T]
which is unique, even in the class of mild solutions.

Corollary 2
.

For problem (
1.8
), the notions of weak and mild solution coincide.

4.3.
Time-dependent a priori estimates

In this part we develop
L^{\infty}
-estimates for weak solutions of the parabolic problem under minimal assumptions. As always, we will assume
that Assumption
1
holds.

To begin, following the same approach as in the end of subsection
3.2
,
given
u\in L^{\infty}(-T,0;L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(-T,0;H^{1}(\Omega))
and
k\geq 0
a fixed real number, we put

u_{k}(t):=(u(t)-k)^{+}.

(4.6)

Clearly
u_{k}(t)\in L^{\infty}(-T,0;L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(-T,0;H^{1}(\Omega))
,
with
\partial_{x_{i}}u_{k}(t)=(\partial_{x_{i}}u(t))\chi_{{}_{\Omega_{k}(t)}}
for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
, where

E_{k}(t):=\{x\in\overline{\Omega}\mid u(t)\geq k\}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,D_{k}(t):=D\cap E_{k}(t),

(4.7)

for each
D\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{N}
such that
D\cap\overline{\Omega}\neq\emptyset
. Then, we have the following assumption:

(A3)’

K(u(t),u_{k}(t))\geq 0
for each
u(t)\in L^{\infty}(-T,0;L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(-T,0;H^{1}(\Omega))
;

(B4)’

For each
u(t)\in L^{\infty}(-T,0;L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(-T,0;D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}}))
one has

u_{k}(t)\in L^{\infty}(-T,0;L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(-T,0;D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}}))
and

\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}}(u(t),u_{k}(t))\,\geq\,c^{\star}_{1}\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}}(u_{k}(t),u_{k}(t))+

+c^{\star}_{2}\|u_{k}(\xi)\|^{2}_{{}_{D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}})}}-c^{\star}_{3}\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma_{k}(t)}\rho(x)(|u_{k}(t)|^{2}+k^{2})\,d\mu,

for some constants
c^{\star}_{1},\,c^{\star}_{2},\,c^{\star}_{3}\geq 0
, and for some function
\rho\in L^{\theta}_{\mu}(\Gamma)
,
where
\theta\geq 1
is such that the map

\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)\hookrightarrow L^{{}^{\frac{2\theta}{\theta-1}}}_{\mu}(\Gamma)
is compact.

In views of
(B4)’
, for each
u(t)\in L^{\infty}(-T,0;L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(-T,0;\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma))
,
we have
u_{k}(t)\in L^{\infty}(-T,0;L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(-T,0;\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma))
. Then for convenience, given
\varsigma>0
, we write

\Upsilon^{2}_{\varsigma}(u):=\displaystyle\sup_{-\varsigma\leq t\leq 0}\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u(t)|^{2}\,dx+\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u(t)|^{2}\,dxdt+\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}\|u(t)\|^{2}_{{}_{D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}})}}\,dt.

(4.8)

By virtue of (
2.5
) and (
2.6
) (Cf.
[
62
, II.3]
in the case of
\Omega
bounded Lipschitz), we get that

\|u\|_{{}_{L^{r_{1}}(-\varsigma,0;L^{s_{1}}(\Omega))}}+\|u\|_{{}_{L^{r_{2}}(-\varsigma,0;L^{s_{2}}_{\mu}(\Gamma))}}\,\leq\,C_{0}\,\Upsilon^{2}_{\varsigma}(u)

(4.9)

for all
u(t)\in L^{\infty}(-T,0;L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(-T,0;\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma))
and for some constant
C_{0}>0
, where

r_{1}\in[2,\infty)
,
s_{1}\in[2,2N(N-2)^{-1}]
,
r_{2}\in[2,\infty)
,
s_{2}\in[2,2d(N-2)^{-1}]
, are such that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r_{1}}+\displaystyle\frac{N}{2s_{1}}=\displaystyle\frac{N}{4}\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{and}\,\,\,\,\,\displaystyle\frac{1}{r_{2}}+\displaystyle\frac{d}{2s_{2}}=\displaystyle\frac{N}{4}.

Lemma 3
.

Given
T>0
fixed, let
u\in L^{\infty}(-T,0;L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(-T,0;\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma))
, and assume that

u_{k}(t)\in L^{\infty}(-T,0;L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(-T,0;D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}}))
. Given
K_{1},\,K_{2}\geq 1
integers and

\xi\in\{1,\ldots,K_{1}\}
, and
\zeta\in\{1,\ldots,K_{2}\}
, let
r_{1,\xi}\in[2,\infty)
,
s_{1,\xi}\in[2,2N(N-2)^{-1}]
,

r_{2,\zeta}\in[2,\infty)
,
s_{2,\zeta}\in[2,2d(N-2)^{-1}]
, such that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r_{1,\xi}}+\displaystyle\frac{N}{2s_{1,\xi}}=\displaystyle\frac{N}{4}\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{and}\,\,\,\,\,\displaystyle\frac{1}{r_{2,\zeta}}+\displaystyle\frac{d}{2s_{2,\zeta}}=\displaystyle\frac{N}{4}.

(4.10)

Assume that there exists constants
\hat{k}_{0},\,\gamma_{0}\geq 0
and positive numbers
\theta_{1,\xi},\,\theta_{2,\zeta}
, such that
for all
\varsigma\in(0,T]
,
\varepsilon\in(0,1/2)
, and
k\geq\hat{k}_{0}
, we have

\Upsilon^{2}_{(1-\varepsilon)\varsigma}(u_{k})\,\leq\,\displaystyle\frac{\gamma_{0}}{\varepsilon\varsigma}\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u_{k}(t)|^{2}\,dx\,dt+

+\gamma_{0}k^{2}\displaystyle\sum^{K_{1}}_{\xi=1}\left(\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}|\Omega_{k}(t)|^{{}^{\frac{r_{1,\xi}}{s_{1,\xi}}}}\,dt\right)^{{}^{\frac{2(1+\theta_{1,\xi})}{r_{1,\xi}}}}+\gamma_{0}k^{2}\displaystyle\sum^{K_{2}}_{\zeta=1}\left(\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}\mu(\Gamma_{k}(t))^{{}^{\frac{r_{2,\zeta}}{s_{2,\zeta}}}}\,dt\right)^{{}^{\frac{2(1+\theta_{2,\zeta})}{r_{2,\zeta}}}}.

(4.11)

Then there exists a constant
C^{\ast}_{0}>0
(independent of
u
and
\hat{k}_{0}
) such that

\textrm{ess}\displaystyle\sup_{\left[-\frac{T}{2},0\right]\times\overline{\Omega}}[u]\,\leq\,C^{\ast}_{0}\left(\|u\|^{2}_{{}_{L^{2}(-T,0;L^{2}(\Omega))}}+\hat{k}^{2}_{0}\right)^{1/2}.

(4.12)

Moreover, if instead of (
4.11
) one has

\Upsilon^{2}_{{}_{T}}(u_{k})\,\leq\,\gamma_{0}\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-T}\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u_{k}(t)|^{2}\,dx\,dt+

+\gamma_{0}k^{2}\displaystyle\sum^{K_{1}}_{\xi=1}\left(\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-T}|\Omega_{k}(t)|^{{}^{\frac{r_{1,\xi}}{s_{1,\xi}}}}\,dt\right)^{{}^{\frac{2(1+\theta_{1,\xi})}{r_{1,\xi}}}}+\gamma_{0}k^{2}\displaystyle\sum^{K_{2}}_{\zeta=1}\left(\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-T}\mu(\Gamma_{k}(t))^{{}^{\frac{r_{2,\zeta}}{s_{2,\zeta}}}}\,dt\right)^{{}^{\frac{2(1+\theta_{2,\zeta})}{r_{2,\zeta}}}},

(4.13)

Then there exists a constant
C^{\star}>0
(independent of
u
and
k_{0}
) such that

\textrm{ess}\displaystyle\sup_{\left[-T,0\right]\times\overline{\Omega}}[u]\,\leq\,C^{\ast}_{0}\left(\|u\|^{2}_{{}_{L^{2}(-T,0;L^{2}(\Omega))}}+\hat{k}^{2}_{0}\right)^{1/2}.

(4.14)

Proof.

The proof is a direct modification of
[
80
, Theorem A.2 and Corollary A.3]
to the case of irregular domains. However, for the sake of completeness we provide a complete proof here.
We begin by observing that since
|\Omega_{k}(t)|\leq|\Omega|
and
\mu(\Gamma_{k}(t))\leq\mu(\Gamma)
, it suffices to consider

\theta_{1,\xi}=\theta_{2,\zeta}=\theta:=\min\left\{\min\{\theta_{1,\xi}\mid 1\leq\xi\leq K_{1}\}\cup\min\{\theta_{2,\zeta}\mid 1\leq\zeta\leq K_{2}\}\right\}

for each
\xi\in\{1,\ldots,K_{1}\}
and
\zeta\in\{1,\ldots,K_{2}\}
, provided that
\gamma_{0}
is replaced by a larger constant
\gamma^{\prime}_{0}

(depending on
r_{1,\xi}
,
s_{1,\xi}
,
\theta_{1,\xi}
,
r_{2,\zeta}
,
s_{2,\zeta}
,
\theta_{2,\zeta}
,
T
,
\gamma_{0}
,
|\Omega|
,

\mu(\Gamma)
), since by performing this selection, both inequalities (
4.11
) and (
4.13
) still hold. Then, given
\hat{k}>\hat{k}_{0}\geq 0
fixed,
define the sequences
\{\varsigma_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
,
\{k_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
,
\{y_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
,
\{z_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
of positive real numbers, by:

\varsigma_{n}:=\displaystyle\frac{T}{2}(1+2^{-(n+1)}),\,\,\,\,\,\,k_{n}:=(2-2^{-n})\hat{k},\,\,\,\,\,\,y_{n}:=\displaystyle\frac{1}{\hat{k}^{2}}\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma_{n}}\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u_{k_{n}}(t)|^{2}\,dx\,dt,

(4.15)

and

z_{n}:=\displaystyle\sum^{K_{1}}_{\xi=1}\left(\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma_{n}}|\Omega_{k_{n}}(t)|^{{}^{\frac{r_{1,\xi}}{s_{1,\xi}}}}\,dt\right)^{{}^{\frac{2}{r_{1,\xi}}}}+\displaystyle\sum^{K_{2}}_{\zeta=1}\left(\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma_{n}}\mu(\Gamma_{k_{n}}(t))^{{}^{\frac{r_{2,\zeta}}{s_{2,\zeta}}}}\,dt\right)^{{}^{\frac{2}{r_{2,\zeta}}}}.

(4.16)

Clearly
\varsigma_{n}\in[T/2,T]
and
k_{n}\geq\hat{k}_{0}
for each nonnegative integer
n
. Moreover, it is clear that

|u_{k_{n}}(t)|\geq(k_{n+1}-k_{n})\chi_{{}_{\overline{\Omega}_{k_{n+1}}(t)}},

so taking into account
the definitions of the sequences defined above together with Hölder’s inequality and (
4.9
), we calculate and get that

\hat{k}^{2}y_{n+1}\,\leq\,\left(\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma_{n+1}}|\Omega_{k_{n+1}}(t)|\,dt\right)^{1/N}\left(\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma_{n+1}}\left\{\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left|u_{k_{n+1}}(t)\right|^{{}^{\frac{2N}{N-1}}}\,dx\right\}^{{}^{\frac{N-1}{N}}}dt\right)

\leq\,c\left(\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma_{n+1}}|\Omega_{k_{n+1}}(t)|\,dt\right)^{1/N}\Upsilon^{2}_{\varsigma_{n+1}}(u_{k_{n+1}})\,\leq\,c\left\{(k_{n+1}-k_{n})^{-2}\hat{k}^{2}y_{n}\right\}^{1/N}\Upsilon^{2}_{\varsigma_{n+1}}(u_{k_{n+1}})\,\leq\,c\,4^{n+1}y_{n}^{1/N}\Upsilon^{2}_{\varsigma_{n+1}}(u_{k_{n+1}}),

(4.17)

where
c>0
is a constant that varies from line to line (this will be assumed for the remaining of the proof). In the same way, we get that

4^{-(n+1)}\hat{k}^{2}z_{n+1}\,=\,(k_{n+1}-k_{n})^{2}z_{n+1}

\leq\,\displaystyle\sum^{K_{1}}_{\xi=1}\left(\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma_{n+1}}\left\{\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u_{k_{n}}(t)|^{s_{1,\xi}}\,dx\right\}^{{}^{\frac{r_{1,\xi}}{s_{1,\xi}}}}\,dt\right)^{{}^{\frac{2}{r_{1,\xi}}}}+\displaystyle\sum^{K_{2}}_{\zeta=1}\left(\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma_{n+1}}\left\{\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}|u_{k_{n}}(t)|^{s_{2,\zeta}}\,d\mu\right\}^{{}^{\frac{r_{2,\zeta}}{s_{2,\zeta}}}}\,dt\right)^{{}^{\frac{2}{r_{2,\zeta}}}}\,\leq\,c\,\Upsilon^{2}_{\varsigma_{n+1}}(u_{k_{n}}).

(4.18)

Now, taking
\varsigma=\varsigma_{n}
and
\varepsilon=1-\varsigma_{n+1}/\varsigma_{n}\geq 2^{-(n+3)}
, we apply the assumption (
4.11
)
to obtain that

\Upsilon^{2}_{\varsigma_{n+1}}(u_{k_{n+1}})\,\leq\,\Upsilon^{2}_{\varsigma_{n+1}}(u_{k_{n}})\,\leq\,\frac{\gamma_{0}}{\varepsilon\varsigma_{n}}\hat{k}^{2}y_{n}+\gamma_{0}k^{2}_{n}z^{1+\theta}_{n}\,\leq\,\gamma_{0}2^{n+4}\hat{k}^{2}(T^{-1}+1)(y_{n}+z^{1+\theta}_{n}),

(4.19)

and then combining (
4.17
), (
4.18
), and (
4.19
), we arrive at

y_{n+1}\,\leq\,c^{\prime}\,8^{n}(y^{1+\delta}_{n}+z^{1+\theta}_{n}y^{\delta}_{n})\,\,\textrm{and}\,\,z_{n+1}\,\leq\,c^{\prime}\,8^{n}(y_{n}+z^{1+\theta}_{n}),\,\,\textrm{for each integer}\,\,n\geq 0,

(4.20)

for some constant
c^{\prime}>0
, where
\delta:=1/N
. To complete the arguments, we seek estimations for
y_{0}
and
z_{0}
. In fact, we easy see that

y_{0}=\displaystyle\frac{1}{\hat{k}^{2}}\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\frac{3T}{4}}\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u_{k_{0}}(t)|^{2}\,dx\,dt\,\leq\,\displaystyle\frac{1}{\hat{k}^{2}}\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-T}\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u(t)|^{2}\,dx\,dt.

(4.21)

On the other hand, using (
4.11
) and following the approach as in (
4.17
) and (
4.18
), we have that

(\hat{k}-\hat{k}_{0})^{2}z_{0}\,\leq\,\displaystyle\sum^{K_{1}}_{\xi=1}\left(\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma_{0}}\left\{\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u_{k_{0}}(t)|^{s_{1,\xi}}\,dx\right\}^{{}^{\frac{r_{1,\xi}}{s_{1,\xi}}}}\,dt\right)^{{}^{\frac{2}{r_{1,\xi}}}}+\displaystyle\sum^{K_{2}}_{\zeta=1}\left(\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma_{0}}\left\{\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}|u_{k_{0}}(t)|^{s_{2,\zeta}}\,d\mu\right\}^{{}^{\frac{r_{2,\zeta}}{s_{2,\zeta}}}}\,dt\right)^{{}^{\frac{2}{r_{2,\zeta}}}}

\leq\,c\,\Upsilon^{2}_{\varsigma_{0}}(u_{k_{0}})\leq\,\displaystyle\frac{4c\gamma_{0}}{T}\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-T}\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u_{k_{0}}(t)|^{2}\,dx\,dt+c\gamma_{0}k^{2}_{0}\displaystyle\sum^{K_{1}}_{\xi=1}\left[T|\Omega|^{{}^{\frac{r_{1,\xi}}{s_{1,\xi}}}}\right]^{{}^{\frac{2(1+\theta)}{r_{1,\xi}}}}+\,c\gamma_{0}k^{2}_{0}\displaystyle\sum^{K_{2}}_{\zeta=1}\left[T\mu(\Gamma)^{{}^{\frac{r_{2,\zeta}}{s_{2,\zeta}}}}\right]^{{}^{\frac{2(1+\theta)}{r_{2,\zeta}}}}.

Thus letting

c^{\prime\prime}:=c\gamma_{0}\max\left\{\frac{4}{T}\,,\,\displaystyle\sum^{K_{1}}_{\xi=1}\left[T|\Omega|^{{}^{\frac{r_{1,\xi}}{s_{1,\xi}}}}\right]^{{}^{\frac{2(1+\theta)}{r_{1,\xi}}}}+\displaystyle\sum^{K_{2}}_{\zeta=1}\left[T\mu(\Gamma)^{{}^{\frac{r_{2,\zeta}}{s_{2,\zeta}}}}\right]^{{}^{\frac{2(1+\theta)}{r_{2,\zeta}}}}\right\},

we arrive at

z_{0}\,\leq\,\displaystyle\frac{c^{\prime\prime}}{(\hat{k}-\hat{k}_{0})^{2}}\left(\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-T}\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u_{k_{0}}(t)|^{2}\,dx\,dt+k^{2}_{0}\right),\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{for all}\,\,\,\hat{k}\geq k_{0}.

(4.22)

Then, we define

d:=\min\left\{\delta,\,\frac{\theta}{1+\theta}\right\}\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{and}\,\,\,\,\,\eta:=\min\left\{\frac{1}{(2c^{\prime})^{{}^{\frac{1}{\delta}}}8^{{}^{\frac{1}{\delta d}}}},\,\frac{1}{(2c^{\prime})^{{}^{\frac{1+\theta}{\theta}}}\,8^{{}^{\frac{1}{\theta d}}}}\right\},

and then choose

\hat{k}:=\max\left\{\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\eta}}\left(\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-T}\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u(t)|^{2}\,dx\,dt\right)^{1/2},\,\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{c_{1}}}{\eta^{{}^{\frac{1}{2(1+\theta)}}}}\left(\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-T}\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u(t)|^{2}\,dx\,dt+\hat{k}^{2}_{0}\right)^{1/2}+\hat{k}_{0}\right\}.

(4.23)

Then one sees that

\hat{k}\,\leq\,c^{\star}\left(\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-T}\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u(t)|^{2}\,dx\,dt+\hat{k}^{2}_{0}\right)^{1/2}

(4.24)

for some constant
c^{\star}>0
, and moreover the selection implies that

y_{0}\,\leq\,\eta\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,z_{0}\,\leq\,\eta^{{}^{\frac{1}{1+\theta}}}.

(4.25)

In views of (
4.20
) and (
4.25
), we see that the sequences
\{y_{n}\}
,
\{z_{n}\}
satisfy the conditions of Lemma
1
.
Thus, applying Lemma
1
for
\hat{k}
given by (
4.23
)
shows that
\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}z_{n}=0
, and consequently

u(t)\,\leq\,\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}k_{n}=2\hat{k}\,\,\,\,\textrm{a.e. in}\,\,\overline{\Omega},\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{for a.e.}\,\,t\in\displaystyle\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N\!}}[-\varsigma_{n},0]=[-T/2,0].

(4.26)

Combining (
4.26
) with (
4.24
), we get that

\displaystyle\textrm{ess}\hskip-19.91684pt\sup_{(t,x)\in\left[-\frac{T}{2},0\right]\times\Omega}\{u(t,x)\}+\displaystyle\textrm{ess}\hskip-17.07182pt\sup_{(t,x)\in\left[-\frac{T}{2},0\right]\times\Gamma}\{u(t,x)\}\,\leq\,C^{\ast}\left(\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-T}\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u(t)|^{2}\,dx\,dt+\hat{k}_{0}\right)^{1/2}.

(4.27)

This gives (
4.12
). To complete the proof,
assume now that (
4.13
) is valid. Then by selecting
\varsigma_{n}:=T
in the previous arguments, the proof runs in the exact way as in the previous case.
In particular, one has that (
4.26
) is valid for almost all
t\in\displaystyle\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N\!}}[-\varsigma_{n},0]=[-T,0]
, leading to
the inequality

\displaystyle\textrm{ess}\hskip-17.92537pt\sup_{(t,x)\in\left[-T,0\right]\times\Omega}\{u(t,x)\}+\displaystyle\textrm{ess}\hskip-17.07182pt\sup_{(t,x)\in\left[-T,0\right]\times\Gamma}\{u(t,x)\}\,\leq\,C^{\star}\left(\displaystyle\int^{T}_{0}\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u(t)|^{2}\,dx\,dt+\hat{k}_{0}\right)^{1/2}.

(4.28)

This completes the proof.

\Box

We now establish a key result concerning the global regularity (in space) of weak solutions of problem (
1.8
).
In time, we also derive local estimates. The proof is motivated by the procedures in
[
80
]
, but as our problem contains
multiple generalizations and additional nonlocal maps and multiple boundary conditions, complete proofs will be provided in order to
deal with the corresponding modifications.

Theorem 6
.

Assume that conditions
(A3)’
and
(B4)’
hold.
Given
T>0
fixed, let
\kappa_{1},\,\kappa_{2},\,p,\,q\in[2,\infty)
be such that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\kappa_{1}}+\displaystyle\frac{N}{2p}<1\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\displaystyle\frac{1}{\kappa_{2}}+\displaystyle\frac{d}{2q(d+2-N)}<\frac{1}{2}.

(4.29)

Given
u_{0}\in L^{2}(\Omega)
, let
f\in L^{\kappa_{1}}(0,T;L^{p}(\Omega))
and
g\in L^{\kappa_{2}}(0,T;L^{q}_{\mu}(\Gamma))
, and
let
u\in C(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(0,T;\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma))
be a weak solution of problem (
1.8
).
Then there exists a constant
C^{\ast}_{1}=C^{\ast}_{1}(T,N,d,\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},p,q,|\Omega|,\mu(\Gamma))>0
such that

|\|\mathbf{u}\||_{{}_{L^{\infty}(T/2,T;\mathbb{X\!}^{\,\infty}(\Omega;\Gamma))}}\,\leq\,C^{\ast}_{1}\left(\|u\|_{{}_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))}}+\|f\|_{{}_{L^{\kappa_{1}}(0,T;L^{p}(\Omega))}}+\|g\|_{{}_{L^{\kappa_{2}}(0,T;L^{q}_{\mu}(\Gamma))}}\right).

(4.30)

If in addition
u_{0}=0
, then we get the global estimate

|\|\mathbf{u}\||_{{}_{L^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{X\!}^{\,\infty}(\Omega;\Gamma))}}\,\leq\,C^{\ast}_{1}\left(\|u\|_{{}_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))}}+\|f\|_{{}_{L^{\kappa_{1}}(0,T;L^{p}(\Omega))}}+\|g\|_{{}_{L^{\kappa_{2}}(0,T;L^{q}_{\mu}(\Gamma))}}\right).

(4.31)

Proof.

Let
u\in C(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(0,T;\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma))
be a weak solution of problem (
1.8
).
The proof of this result is technical and long, and will be divided into three main steps.

\bullet

Step 1
. Assume in addition that
u\in C(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(0,T;\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma))

is a classical solution of (
1.8
), and that
T\leq T_{0}
for some constant
T_{0}>0
(depending only in
N
,
d
,
\kappa_{1}
,

\kappa_{2}
,
p
,
q
,
|\Omega|
,
\mu(\Gamma)
, and the coefficients of the operators
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
). By a linear
translation in time, we may assume that problem (
1.8
) is solved over
[-T,0]
, with initial value
u_{0}=u(-T)
.
Let
u_{k}(t)
be the function given by (
4.6
), fix
\varsigma\in[-T,0]
, and select a function
\psi\in H^{1}(-\varsigma,0)

such that
0\leq\psi(t)\leq 1
for all
t\in[-\varsigma,0]
. Assume in addition that either
\psi(-\varsigma)=0
, or
\varsigma=T
and

u_{k}(-T)=0
. Then for each
t\in[-\varsigma,0]
, observe that

\displaystyle\frac{\psi(t)^{2}}{2}\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}(t)}|u_{k}(t)|^{2}\,dx=\displaystyle\int^{t}_{-\varsigma}\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\xi}\left(\displaystyle\frac{\psi(\xi)^{2}}{2}\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}(\xi)}|u_{k}(\xi)|^{2}\,dx\right)d\xi

=\displaystyle\int^{t}_{-\varsigma}\psi(\xi)\psi^{\prime}(\xi)\left(\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}(\xi)}|u_{k}(\xi)|^{2}\,dx\right)d\xi+\displaystyle\int^{t}_{-\varsigma}\psi(\xi)^{2}\left(\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}(\xi)}u_{k}(\xi)\frac{du_{k}(\xi)}{d\xi}\,dx\right)d\xi.

(4.32)

Now, using the fact that
u\in C(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(0,T;\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma))

is a classical solution of (
1.8
), we see that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}(\xi)}u_{k}(\xi)\frac{du_{k}(\xi)}{d\xi}\,dx=\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}(\xi)}u_{k}(\xi)\frac{du(\xi)}{d\xi}\,dx=\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}(\xi)}f(\xi,x)u_{k}(\xi)\,dx+\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma_{k}(\xi)}g(\xi,x)u_{k}(\xi)\,d\mu-\mathcal{E}_{\mu}(u(\xi),u_{k}(\xi)),

(4.33)

where we recall the definition of the form
\mathcal{E}_{\mu}(\cdot,\cdot)
in (
3.1
). Estimating the right hand side in (
4.33
) and taking
into account Assumptions
(A3)’
and
(B4)’
, we see that

\mathcal{E}_{\mu}(u(\xi),u_{k}(\xi))\,\geq\,\mathcal{E}_{{}_{\Omega}}(u_{k}(\xi),u_{k}(\xi))+\min\{1,c^{\star}_{1}\}\mathcal{E}_{{}_{\Gamma}}(u_{k}(\xi),u_{k}(\xi))+

+\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}(\xi)}\displaystyle\sum^{N}_{i=1}\hat{a}_{i}k\partial_{x_{i}}u_{k}(\xi)\,dx+\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}(\xi)}\lambda ku_{k}(\xi)\,dx+\min\{1,c^{\star}_{1}\}\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma_{k}(\xi)}\beta ku_{k}(\xi)\,d\mu+

+c^{\star}_{2}\|u_{k}(\xi)\|^{2}_{{}_{D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}})}}-c^{\star}_{3}\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma_{k}(t)}\rho(x)(|u_{k}(t)|^{2}+k^{2})\,d\mu,

(4.34)

where the constants
c^{\star}_{m}
(
m\in\{1,2,3\}
) and the function
\rho
are described in condition
(B4)’
.
Recalling (
1.4
), we apply Young’s inequality multiple times and use Remark
2
(d) to produce the following estimates.

c_{0}\|\nabla u_{k}(\xi)\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega_{k}(\xi)}}\,\leq\,\mathcal{E}_{{}_{\Omega}}(u_{k}(\xi),u_{k}(\xi))+\|\hat{\mathfrak{a}}\|_{{}_{r_{1},\Omega}}\|u_{k}(\xi)\|_{{}_{\frac{2r_{1}}{r_{1}-2},\Omega_{k}(\xi)}}\|\nabla u_{k}(\xi)\|_{{}_{2,\Omega_{k}(\xi)}}+

\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,+\|\check{\mathfrak{a}}\|_{{}_{r_{2},\Omega}}\|u_{k}(\xi)\|_{{}_{\frac{2r_{2}}{r_{2}-2},\Omega_{k}(\xi)}}\|\nabla u_{k}(\xi)\|_{{}_{2,\Omega_{k}(\xi)}}+\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}(\xi)}|\lambda||u_{k}(\xi)|^{2}\,dx

\leq\,\mathcal{E}_{{}_{\Omega}}(u_{k}(\xi),u_{k}(\xi))+\|\hat{\mathfrak{a}}\|_{{}_{r_{1},\Omega}}\left(2\epsilon_{1}\|\nabla u_{k}(\xi)\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega_{k}(\xi)}}+\frac{C_{\epsilon^{2}_{1}}}{\epsilon_{1}}\|u_{k}(\xi)\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega_{k}(\xi)}}\right)+\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,

+\|\check{\mathfrak{a}}\|_{{}_{r_{2},\Omega}}\left(2\epsilon_{1}\|\nabla u_{k}(\xi)\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega_{k}(\xi)}}+\frac{C^{\prime}_{\epsilon^{2}_{1}}}{\epsilon_{1}}\|u_{k}(\xi)\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega_{k}(\xi)}}\right)+\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}(\xi)}|\lambda||u_{k}(\xi)|^{2}\,dx,

(4.35)

for all
\epsilon_{1}>0
, for some constant
C_{\epsilon^{2}_{1}},\,C^{\prime}_{\epsilon^{2}_{1}}>0
. Writing
C_{1}:=C_{\epsilon^{2}_{1}}
,
C_{2}=C^{\prime}_{\epsilon^{2}_{1}}
,
and

M^{\star}:=\max\left\{\|\hat{\mathfrak{a}}\|_{{}_{r_{1},\Omega}},\|\check{\mathfrak{a}}\|_{{}_{r_{2},\Omega}}\right\}\left(C_{1}\|\hat{\mathfrak{a}}\|_{{}_{r_{1},\Omega}}+C_{2}\|\check{\mathfrak{a}}\|_{{}_{r_{2},\Omega}}\right)/c_{0}>0,

we insert
\epsilon_{1}:=c_{0}\left(8\max\left\{\|\hat{\mathfrak{a}}\|_{{}_{r_{1},\Omega}},\|\check{\mathfrak{a}}\|_{{}_{r_{2},\Omega}}\right\}\right)^{-1}

into (
4.35
) to get that

\mathcal{E}_{{}_{\Omega}}(u_{k}(\xi),u_{k}(\xi))\,\geq\,\displaystyle\frac{c_{0}}{2}\|\nabla u_{k}(\xi)\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega_{k}(\xi)}}-\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}(\xi)}(M^{\star}+|\lambda|)|u_{k}(\xi)|^{2}\,dx.

(4.36)

Also, recalling that the “Wentzell-type” boundary form
(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}},D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}}))
is assumed to be weakly coercive,
we get the existence of constants
c^{\ast}_{1}>0
and
c^{\ast}_{2}\in\mathbb{R\!}\,
such that

\mathcal{E}_{{}_{\Gamma}}(u_{k}(\xi),u_{k}(\xi))\,\geq\,c^{\ast}_{1}\|u_{k}(\xi)\|^{2}_{{}_{D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}})}}-\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma_{k}(\xi)}(|c^{\ast}_{2}|+|\beta|)|u_{k}(\xi)|^{2}\,d\mu.

(4.37)

It is also easy to see that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}(\xi)}\displaystyle\sum^{N}_{i=1}\hat{a}_{i}k\partial_{x_{i}}u_{k}(\xi)\,dx\,\geq\,-\displaystyle\frac{k^{2}}{c_{0}}\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}(\xi)}\displaystyle\sum^{N}_{i=1}|\hat{a}_{i}|^{2}\,dx-\displaystyle\frac{c_{0}}{4}\|\nabla u_{k}(\xi)\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega_{k}(\xi)}},

(4.38)

and

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}(\xi)}f(\xi,x)u_{k}(\xi)\,dx+\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma_{k}(\xi)}g(\xi,x)u_{k}(\xi)\,d\mu

\leq\,\displaystyle\frac{1}{k}\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}(\xi)}|f(\xi,x)|(|u_{k}(\xi)|^{2}+k^{2})\,dx+\displaystyle\frac{1}{k}\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma_{k}(\xi)}|g(\xi,x)|(|u_{k}(\xi)|^{2}+k^{2})\,d\mu.

(4.39)

Letting

\mathfrak{h}:=\frac{1}{c_{0}}\displaystyle\sum^{N}_{i=1}|\hat{a}_{i}|^{2}+M^{\star}+|\lambda|\in L^{{}^{\min\left\{\frac{r_{1}}{2},r_{3}\right\}}}(\Omega)^{+}

and

\mathfrak{k}:=\min\{1,c^{\star}_{1}\}(|c^{\ast}_{2}|+2|\beta|)+c^{\star}_{3}|\rho|\in L^{{}^{\min\{s,\theta\}}}_{\mu}(\Gamma)^{+},

we insert (
4.36
), (
4.37
), and (
4.38
) into (
4.34
), to deduce that

\mathcal{E}_{\mu}(u(\xi),u_{k}(\xi))\,\geq\,\displaystyle\frac{c_{0}}{4}\|\nabla u_{k}(\xi)\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega_{k}(\xi)}}+c^{\ast\star}\|u_{k}(\xi)\|^{2}_{{}_{D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}})}}

-\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}(\xi)}\mathfrak{h}(|u_{k}(\xi)|^{2}+k^{2})\,dx-\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma_{k}(\xi)}\mathfrak{k}(|u_{k}(\xi)|^{2}+k^{2})\,d\mu,

(4.40)

where
c^{\ast\star}:=c^{\ast}_{1}\min\{1,c^{\star}_{1}\}+c^{\star}_{2}
.
Substituting this into (
4.33
) and recalling (
4.39
) gives that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}(\xi)}u_{k}(\xi)\frac{du_{k}(\xi)}{d\xi}\,dx\,\leq\,-\displaystyle\frac{c_{0}}{4}\|\nabla u_{k}(\xi)\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega_{k}(\xi)}}-c^{\ast\star}\|u_{k}(\xi)\|^{2}_{{}_{D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}})}}

+\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}(\xi)}\left(\frac{1}{k}|f(\xi,x)|+\mathfrak{h}\right)(|u_{k}(\xi)|^{2}+k^{2})\,dx+\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma_{k}(\xi)}\left(\frac{1}{k}|g(\xi,x)|+\mathfrak{k}\right)(|u_{k}(\xi)|^{2}+k^{2})\,d\mu.

(4.41)

From here, we insert (
4.41
) into (
4.32
) to get that

\min\left\{\displaystyle\frac{1}{2},\displaystyle\frac{c_{0}}{4},c^{\ast\star}\right\}\Upsilon^{2}_{\varsigma}(\psi u_{k})\,\leq\,\displaystyle\sup_{-\varsigma\leq t\leq 0}\left(\displaystyle\frac{\psi(t)^{2}}{2}\|u_{k}(t)\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega_{k}(t)}}\right)+\frac{c_{0}}{4}\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}\psi(t)^{2}\|\nabla u_{k}(t)\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega_{k}(t)}}\,dt\,+\,c^{\ast\star}\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}\psi(t)^{2}\|u_{k}(t)\|^{2}_{{}_{D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}})}}\,dt

\leq\,L_{\psi^{\prime}}\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}\|u_{k}(t)\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega_{k}(t)}}\,dt+\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}(t)}\left(\frac{1}{k}|f(t,x)|+\mathfrak{h}\right)(\psi(t)^{2}|u_{k}(t)|^{2}+k^{2})\,dx\,dt+\,\,\,\,\,

\indent\indent\indent\indent+\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma_{k}(t)}\left(\frac{1}{k}|g(t,x)|+\mathfrak{k}\right)(\psi(t)^{2}|u_{k}(t)|^{2}+k^{2})\,d\mu\,dt,

(4.42)

where
L_{\psi^{\prime}}:=\displaystyle\sup_{-\varsigma\leq t\leq 0}|\psi^{\prime}(t)|
. We now seek to estimate the last two terms in (
4.42
). For this,
we choose

\theta_{1}:=\frac{4\tilde{p}+2\tilde{\kappa}_{1}N-\tilde{p}\tilde{\kappa}_{1}N}{\tilde{p}\tilde{\kappa}_{1}N}>0\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{and}\,\,\,\,\,\theta_{2}:=\frac{4\tilde{q}+2\tilde{\kappa}_{2}d-\tilde{q}\tilde{\kappa}_{2}N}{\tilde{q}\tilde{\kappa}_{2}N}>0,

where
\tilde{\tau}:=2\tau(\tau-1)^{-1}
for
\tau\in(1,\infty)
. Then

\frac{1}{\tilde{\kappa}_{1}(1+\theta_{1})}+\frac{N}{2\tilde{p}(1+\theta_{1})}=\frac{1}{\tilde{\kappa}_{2}(1+\theta_{2})}+\frac{d}{2\tilde{q}(1+\theta_{2})}=\frac{N}{4}.

Put

\hat{k}^{2}_{0}:=\|f\|^{2}_{{}_{L^{\kappa_{1}}(0,T;L^{p}(\Omega))}}+\|g\|^{2}_{{}_{L^{\kappa_{2}}(0,T;L^{q}_{\mu}(\Gamma))}},

(4.43)

and apply Hölder’s inequality to deduce that

\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}(t)}\frac{1}{k}|f(t,x)|\psi(t)^{2}|u_{k}(t)|^{2}\,dx\,dt\,\leq\,\displaystyle\frac{1}{k}\|f\|_{{}_{L^{\kappa_{1}}(-\varsigma,0;L^{p}(\Omega))}}\|\psi u_{k}\|^{2}_{{}_{L^{\tilde{\kappa}_{1}}(-\varsigma,0;L^{\tilde{p}}(\Omega))}}

\leq\,\displaystyle\frac{\hat{k}_{0}}{k}\left(\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}|\Omega_{k}(t)|^{\tilde{\kappa}_{1}/\tilde{p}}dt\right)^{{}^{\frac{2\kappa_{1}\theta_{1}}{1+\theta_{1}}}}\|\psi u_{k}\|^{2}_{{}_{L^{\tilde{\kappa}_{1}(1+\theta_{1})}(-\varsigma,0;L^{\tilde{p}(1+\theta_{1})}(\Omega))}}.

(4.44)

Now observe that

\displaystyle\lim_{\varsigma\rightarrow 0^{+}}\left\{\left(\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}|\Omega_{k}(t)|^{\tilde{\kappa}_{1}/\tilde{p}}dt\right)^{{}^{\frac{2\kappa_{1}\theta_{1}}{1+\theta_{1}}}}\|\psi u_{k}\|^{2}_{{}_{L^{\tilde{\kappa}_{1}(1+\theta_{1})}(-\varsigma,0;L^{\tilde{p}(1+\theta_{1})}(\Omega))}}\right\}=0,

so using this together with the selection of
\theta_{1}
and (
4.9
) entail the existence of a constant

T_{1}=T_{1}(N,\kappa_{1},\theta_{1},p,|\Omega|)>0
such that

\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}(t)}\frac{1}{k}|f(t,x)|\psi(t)^{2}|u_{k}(t)|^{2}\,dx\,dt\,\leq\,\displaystyle\frac{\hat{k}_{0}}{8k}\min\left\{\displaystyle\frac{1}{2},\displaystyle\frac{c_{0}}{4},c^{\ast\star}\right\}\Upsilon^{2}_{\varsigma}(\psi u_{k})

(4.45)

whenever
\varsigma\leq T_{1}
. Also, since
\mathfrak{h}\in L^{{}^{\min\left\{\frac{r_{1}}{2},r_{3}\right\}}}(\Omega)
and

\mathfrak{r}:=2\min\{r_{1}/2,\,r_{3}\}\left(\min\{r_{1}/2,\,r_{3}\}-1\right)^{-1}<2N(N-2)^{-1},

proceeding in the same way as in the derivation of (
4.45
), we get the existence of a constant

T_{2}=T_{2}(N,\min\{r_{1}/2,\,r_{3}\},\mathfrak{h},|\Omega|)>0
fulfilling

\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}(t)}\mathfrak{h}\psi(t)^{2}|u_{k}(t)|^{2}\,dx\,dt\,\leq\,\|\mathfrak{h}\|_{{}_{\min\left\{\frac{r_{1}}{2},r_{3}\right\},\Omega}}\|\psi u_{k}\|^{2}_{{}_{L^{2}(-\varsigma,0;L^{\mathfrak{r}}(\Omega))}}\,\leq\,\displaystyle\frac{1}{8}\min\left\{\displaystyle\frac{1}{2},\displaystyle\frac{c_{0}}{4},c^{\ast\star}\right\}\Upsilon^{2}_{\varsigma}(\psi u_{k})

(4.46)

for
\varsigma\leq T_{2}
. Moreover, recalling the selection of
\theta_{2}
, and using the fact that
\mathfrak{k}\in L^{{}^{\min\{s,\theta\}}}_{\mu}(\Gamma)
with

\mathfrak{s}:=2\min\{s,\theta\}\left(\min\{s,\theta\}-1\right)^{-1}<2d(N-2)^{-1},

we proceed in the exact way as above to deduce the existence
of positive constants
T_{3}=T_{3}(N,d,\kappa_{2},\theta_{2},q,\mu(\Gamma))
and
T_{4}=T_{4}(N,d,\min\{s,\theta\},\mathfrak{k},\mu(\Gamma))
such that

\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma_{k}(t)}\frac{1}{k}|g(t,x)|\psi(t)^{2}|u_{k}(t)|^{2}\,d\mu\,dt\,\leq\,\displaystyle\frac{\hat{k}_{0}}{8k}\min\left\{\displaystyle\frac{1}{2},\displaystyle\frac{c_{0}}{4},c^{\ast\star}\right\}\Upsilon^{2}_{\varsigma}(\psi u_{k}),

(4.47)

if
\varsigma\leq T_{3}
, and

\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma_{k}(t)}\mathfrak{k}\psi(t)^{2}|u_{k}(t)|^{2}\,d\mu\,dt\,\leq\,\|\mathfrak{k}\|_{{}_{\min\{s,\theta\},\Gamma}}\|\psi u_{k}\|^{2}_{{}_{L^{2}(-\varsigma,0;L^{\mathfrak{s}}_{\mu}(\Gamma))}}\,\leq\,\displaystyle\frac{1}{8}\min\left\{\displaystyle\frac{1}{2},\displaystyle\frac{c_{0}}{4},c^{\ast\star}\right\}\Upsilon^{2}_{\varsigma}(\psi u_{k}),

(4.48)

whenever
\varsigma\leq T_{4}
. Letting
T_{0}:=\min\{T_{1},T_{2},T_{3},T_{4}\}
, and taking
\varsigma\in(0,T_{0}]
and
k\geq\hat{k}_{0}
, we insert
(
4.45
), (
4.46
), (
4.47
), and (
4.48
) into (
4.42
), to arrive at

\Upsilon^{2}_{\varsigma}(\psi u_{k})\,\leq\,\displaystyle\frac{2}{\min\{1/2,c_{0}/4,c^{\ast\star}\}}\left\{L_{\psi^{\prime}}\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}\|u_{k}(t)\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega_{k}(t)}}\,dt\right.+

+\left.k^{2}\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}(t)}\left(\frac{1}{k}|f(t,x)|+\mathfrak{h}\right)\,dx\,dt\,+\,k^{2}\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma_{k}(t)}\left(\frac{1}{k}|g(t,x)|+\mathfrak{k}\right)\,d\mu\,dt\right\}.

(4.49)

Having this, we now produce estimates in the last two integral terms in (
4.49
). In fact,
recalling that
k\geq\hat{k}_{0}
and applying Hölder’s inequality, we see that

\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}(t)}\frac{1}{k}|f(t,x)|\,dx\,dt\,\leq\,\displaystyle\frac{1}{k}\|f\|_{{}_{L^{\kappa_{1}}(-\varsigma,0;L^{p}(\Omega))}}\left(\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}|\Omega_{k}(t)|^{{}^{\frac{\kappa_{1}(p-1)}{p(\kappa_{1}-1)}}}dt\right)^{{}^{\frac{\kappa_{1}-1}{\kappa_{1}}}}\,\leq\,\left(\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}|\Omega_{k}(t)|^{{}^{\frac{\kappa_{1}(p-1)}{p(\kappa_{1}-1)}}}dt\right)^{{}^{\frac{\kappa_{1}-1}{\kappa_{1}}}}.

(4.50)

Proceeding in the exact way, we find that

\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}(t)}\mathfrak{h}\,dx\,dt\,\leq\,\|\mathfrak{h}\|_{{}_{\min\left\{\frac{r_{1}}{2},r_{3}\right\},\Omega}}\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}|\Omega_{k}(t)|^{2/\mathfrak{r}}dt,

(4.51)

and

\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma_{k}(t)}\frac{1}{k}|g(t,x)|\,d\mu\,dt\,\leq\,\left(\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}\mu(\Gamma_{k}(t))^{{}^{\frac{\kappa_{2}(q-1)}{q(\kappa_{2}-1)}}}dt\right)^{{}^{\frac{\kappa_{2}-1}{\kappa_{2}}}},

(4.52)

and

\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma_{k}(t)}\mathfrak{k}\,d\mu\,dt\,\leq\,\|\mathfrak{k}\|_{{}_{\min\{s,\theta\},\Gamma}}\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}\mu(\Gamma_{k}(t))^{2/\mathfrak{s}}dt.

(4.53)

Put

\mathfrak{c}_{1}:=\displaystyle\frac{2}{\min\{1/2,c_{0}/4,c^{\ast\star}\}}\,\,\,\textrm{and}\,\,\,\mathfrak{c}_{2}:=\displaystyle\frac{2\max\left\{1,\|\mathfrak{h}\|_{{}_{\min\{r_{1},r_{3}\},\Omega}},\|\mathfrak{k}\|_{{}_{\min\{s,\theta\},\Gamma}}\right\}}{\min\{1/2,c_{0}/4,c^{\ast\star}\}},

and we choose

\theta_{1,1}:=\frac{4\tilde{p}+2\tilde{\kappa}_{1}N-\tilde{p}\tilde{\kappa}_{1}N}{\tilde{p}\tilde{\kappa}_{1}N},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,r_{1,1}:=(1+\theta_{1,1})\tilde{\kappa}_{1},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,s_{1,1}:=(1+\theta_{1,1})\tilde{p},

\theta_{1,2}:=\frac{2N+2\mathfrak{r}-N\mathfrak{r}}{N\mathfrak{r}},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,r_{1,2}:=2(1+\theta_{1,2}),\,\,\,\,\,\,\,s_{1,2}:=(1+\theta_{1,2})\mathfrak{r},

\theta_{2,1}:=\frac{4\tilde{q}+2\tilde{\kappa}_{2}d-\tilde{q}\tilde{\kappa}_{2}N}{\tilde{q}\tilde{\kappa}_{2}N},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,r_{2,1}:=(1+\theta_{2,1})\tilde{\kappa}_{2},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,s_{2,1}:=(1+\theta_{2,1})\tilde{q},

\theta_{2,2}:=\frac{2d+2\mathfrak{s}-N\mathfrak{s}}{N\mathfrak{s}},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,r_{2,2}:=2(1+\theta_{2,2}),\,\,\,\,\,\,\,s_{2,2}:=(1+\theta_{2,2})\mathfrak{s}.

A straightforward calculation shows that the parameters
\theta_{1,\xi},\,\theta_{2,\zeta},\,r_{1,\xi},\,r_{2,\zeta},\,s_{1,\xi},\,s_{2,\zeta}

(
\xi,\,\zeta\in\{1,2\}
) fulfill (
4.10
).
Then using all these notations, and substituting (
4.50
), (
4.51
), (
4.52
), and (
4.53
) into (
4.49
), we arrive at

\Upsilon^{2}_{\varsigma}(\psi u)\,\leq\,\mathfrak{c}_{1}L_{\psi^{\prime}}\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}\|u_{k}(t)\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega_{k}(t)}}\,dt+

+\mathfrak{c}_{2}k^{2}\displaystyle\sum^{2}_{\xi=1}\left(\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}|\Omega_{k}(t)|^{{}^{\frac{r_{1,\xi}}{s_{1,\xi}}}}\,dt\right)^{{}^{\frac{2(1+\theta_{1,\xi})}{r_{1,\xi}}}}+\mathfrak{c}_{2}k^{2}\displaystyle\sum^{2}_{\zeta=1}\left(\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}\mu(\Gamma_{k}(t))^{{}^{\frac{r_{2,\zeta}}{s_{2,\zeta}}}}\,dt\right)^{{}^{\frac{2(1+\theta_{2,\zeta})}{r_{2,\zeta}}}}.

(4.54)

Next, given
\varepsilon\in(0,1/2)
fixed, choose

\psi_{\varepsilon}(t):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\displaystyle\frac{t+\varsigma}{\varepsilon\varsigma},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{if}\,\,t\in[-\varsigma,-(1-\varepsilon)\varsigma],\\[8.61108pt]
\,\,\,\,1,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{if}\,\,t\in[-(1-\varepsilon)\varsigma,0].\\
\end{array}\right.

Clearly
\psi_{\varepsilon}\in H^{1}(-\varsigma,0)
with
0\leq\psi_{\varepsilon}\leq 1
,
\psi_{\varepsilon}(-\varsigma)=0
, and

L_{\psi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}}:=\displaystyle\sup_{-\varsigma\leq t\leq 0}|\psi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(t)|\leq(\varepsilon\varsigma)^{-1}
. Moreover, we
obtain that

\Upsilon^{2}_{{}_{(1-\varepsilon)\varsigma}}(u)\,\leq\,\Upsilon^{2}_{\varsigma}(\psi_{\varepsilon}u),

and thus letting
\gamma_{0}:=\max\{\mathfrak{c}_{1},\mathfrak{c}_{2}\}
and replacing
\psi=\psi_{\varepsilon}
in (
4.54
), we obtain that

\Upsilon^{2}_{{}_{(1-\varepsilon)\varsigma}}(u)\,\leq\,\displaystyle\frac{\gamma_{0}}{\varepsilon\varsigma}\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}\|u_{k}(t)\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega_{k}(t)}}\,dt+

+\gamma_{0}k^{2}\displaystyle\sum^{2}_{\xi=1}\left(\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}|\Omega_{k}(t)|^{{}^{\frac{r_{1,\xi}}{s_{1,\xi}}}}\,dt\right)^{{}^{\frac{2(1+\theta_{1,\xi})}{r_{1,\xi}}}}+\gamma_{0}k^{2}\displaystyle\sum^{2}_{\zeta=1}\left(\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}\mu(\Gamma_{k}(t))^{{}^{\frac{r_{2,\zeta}}{s_{2,\zeta}}}}\,dt\right)^{{}^{\frac{2(1+\theta_{2,\zeta})}{r_{2,\zeta}}}}.

(4.55)

Therefore, (
4.55
) shows that (
4.11
) and all the conditions of Lemma
3
are fulfilled, and thus
applying Lemma
3
to both
u
and
-u
(the latter one being a weak solution of (
1.8
) for
f,\,g
replaced by

-f,\,-g
, respectively), we establish (
4.30
) in the case
T\leq T_{0}
.

\bullet

Step 2
. Assume now in addition that
u(-T)=u_{0}=0
. Then by choosing
\varsigma:=T
and
and
\psi(t):=1
for al
t\in[-T,0]
, equality (
4.32
) becomes

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}(t)}|u_{k}(t)|^{2}\,dx=\displaystyle\int^{t}_{-T}\left(\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}(\xi)}u_{k}(\xi)\frac{du_{k}(\xi)}{d\xi}\,dx\right)d\xi.

(4.56)

From here, the calculations follow in the exact way as in the previous step, and in particular (
4.42
) is transformed into

\min\left\{\displaystyle\frac{1}{2},\displaystyle\frac{c_{0}}{4},c^{\ast\star}\right\}\Upsilon^{2}_{{}_{T}}(u)\,\leq\,\displaystyle\sup_{-T\leq t\leq 0}\left(\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|u_{k}(t)\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega_{k}(t)}}\right)+\frac{c_{0}}{4}\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-T}\|\nabla u_{k}(t)\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega_{k}(t)}}\,dt\,+\,c^{\ast\star}\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-T}\|u_{k}(t)\|^{2}_{{}_{D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}})}}\,dt
