\leq\,\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-T}\left(\|u_{k}(t)\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega_{k}(t)}}+\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}(t)}\left(\frac{1}{k}|f(t,x)|+\mathfrak{h}\right)(|u_{k}(t)|^{2}+k^{2})\,dx+\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma_{k}(t)}\left(\frac{1}{k}|g(t,x)|+\mathfrak{k}\right)(|u_{k}(t)|^{2}+k^{2})\,d\mu\right)\,dt.

(4.57)

Continuing analogously as in the previous case, we arrive a the inequality

\Upsilon^{2}_{{}_{T}}(u)\,\leq\,\gamma_{0}\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-T}\|u_{k}(t)\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega_{k}(t)}}\,dt+

+\gamma_{0}k^{2}\displaystyle\sum^{2}_{\xi=1}\left(\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-T}|\Omega_{k}(t)|^{{}^{\frac{r_{1,\xi}}{s_{1,\xi}}}}\,dt\right)^{{}^{\frac{2(1+\theta_{1,\xi})}{r_{1,\xi}}}}+\gamma_{0}k^{2}\displaystyle\sum^{2}_{\zeta=1}\left(\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-T}\mu(\Gamma_{k}(t))^{{}^{\frac{r_{2,\zeta}}{s_{2,\zeta}}}}\,dt\right)^{{}^{\frac{2(1+\theta_{2,\zeta})}{r_{2,\zeta}}}},

(4.58)

where all the constants and parameters appearing in (
4.58
) are defined as in the previous step. Consequently,
(
4.13
) is valid for this case, and another application of Lemma
3
gives (
4.31
).
This completes the proof of Theorem
6
in the case when the weak solution

u\in C(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(0,T;\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma))
of (
1.8
) is also a
classical solution of (
1.8
), and for
T\leq T_{0}
.

\bullet

Step 3
. We now prove the general case. Since the proof runs in a similar way as in

[
80
, proof of Proposition 3.1]
, we will only sketch the main ingredients of the proof. Let

u\in C(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(0,T;\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma))
be a weak solution of problem (
1.8
). Since
D(A_{\mu})
is dense
in
L^{2}(\Omega)
, we pick
\{u_{0,n}\}\subseteq D(A^{2}_{\mu})
such that
u_{0,n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n\rightarrow\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}u_{0}
in
L^{2}(\Omega)
.
We also take sequences
\{f_{n}\}\subseteq C^{2}([0,T];L^{\infty}(\Omega))
and
\{g_{n}\}\subseteq C^{2}([0,T];L^{\infty}_{\mu}(\Gamma))

fulfilling
f_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n\rightarrow\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}f
in
L^{\kappa_{1}}(0,T;L^{p}(\Omega))
and

g_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n\rightarrow\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}g
in
L^{\kappa_{2}}(0,T;L^{q}_{\mu}(\Gamma))
, and
f_{n}(0,x)=g_{n}(0,x)=0
for every
n\in\mathbb{N\!}\,
.
Using
f_{n},\,g_{n}
as the data functions in problem (
1.8
), from an application of Theorem
3
, Theorem
5
,
and Corollary
2
, we get that problem (
1.8
) (with data
f_{n},\,g_{n}
) admits a unique classical solution

u_{n}
, and
u_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n\rightarrow\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}u
in
C([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(0,T;\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma))
.
Then, given
T>0
arbitrary, put
T^{\prime}_{0}:=\min\{T_{0},T\}>0
, for
T_{0}>0
the parameter given in the previous two steps, and let
I^{\prime}\subseteq[T^{\prime}_{0}/2,T^{\prime}_{0}]
be an interval.
Then the inequality (
4.30
) is valid (over
I^{\prime}
) for the function
u_{n}
(with data
f_{n},\,g_{n}
), and moreover an application of (
4.30
) to
the function
u_{n}-u_{m}
(
n,\,m\in\mathbb{N\!}\,
) shows that
\{u_{n}\}
is a Cauchy sequence in
L^{\infty}(I^{\prime};L^{\infty}(\Omega))
. Thus

u_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n\rightarrow\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}u
over
L^{\infty}(I^{\prime};L^{\infty}(\Omega))
. Therefore, passing to the limit and
using the fact that
[T/2,T]\subseteq\displaystyle\bigcup^{m}_{j=1}I^{\prime}_{i}
(for some
m\in\mathbb{N\!}\,
) with
\ell(I^{\prime}_{i})\leq L^{\prime}_{0}/2
, we are lead into the fulfillment
of the inequality (
4.30
). If in addition
u_{0}=0
, then the same procedure is repeated over the inequality (
4.31
), and this
completes the proof of the theorem.

\Box

Now we close this section by providing a priori estimates depending in the initial condition of problem (
1.8
). We begin
with an estimate global in time. In this case, since the initial condition is only assumed to be bounded in
\Omega
, if
u_{0}\neq 0
,
then it is still an open problem to obtain global
L^{\infty}
-estimates in the sense that in this case weak solutions to equation (
1.8
) are only known to be bounded
over
\Omega
(in fact, they are globally bounded whenever
t>0
, but
u_{0}
may fail to be bounded unless specified otherwise).
To be more precise, we have the following result whose proof runs as in
[
80
, proof of Theorem 3.2]
.

Theorem 7
.

Suppose that conditions
(A3)’
and
(B4)’
hold.
Given
T>0
arbitrary fixed, let
\kappa_{1},\,\kappa_{2},\,p,\,q\in[2,\infty)
be such that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\kappa_{1}}+\displaystyle\frac{N}{2p}<1\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\displaystyle\frac{1}{\kappa_{2}}+\displaystyle\frac{d}{2q(d+2-N)}<\frac{1}{2}.

(4.59)

Given
u_{0}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
, let
f\in L^{\kappa_{1}}(0,T;L^{p}(\Omega))
and
g\in L^{\kappa_{2}}(0,T;L^{q}_{\mu}(\Gamma))
, and
let
u\in C(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(0,T;\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma))
be a weak solution of problem (
1.8
).
Then there exists a constant
C^{\ast}_{2}=C^{\ast}_{2}(T,N,d,\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},p,q,|\Omega|,\mu(\Gamma))>0
such that

\|u\|_{{}_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{\infty}(\Omega))}}\,\leq\,C^{\ast}_{2}\left(\|u_{0}\|_{{}_{\infty,\Omega}}+\|f\|_{{}_{L^{\kappa_{1}}(0,T;L^{p}(\Omega))}}+\|g\|_{{}_{L^{\kappa_{2}}(0,T;L^{q}_{\mu}(\Gamma))}}\right).

(4.60)

Proof.

If
u\in C(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(0,T;\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma))
solves (
1.8
), then by linearity one can write
u(t):=T_{\mu}(t)u_{0}+\tilde{u}(t)
,
where
T_{\mu}(t)u_{0}
denotes the weak solution of the homogeneous problem (
1.8
) for
f=g=0
(recall Corollary
2
),
and
\tilde{u}(t)
solves (
1.8
) for
u_{0}=0
. Then an application of (
4.3
) together with (
4.31
) entail that

\|u\|^{2}_{{}_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{\infty}(\Omega))}}\,\leq\,2\left(\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|T_{\mu}(t)u_{0}\|^{2}_{{}_{\infty,\Omega}}+\|\tilde{u}\|^{2}_{{}_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{\infty}(\Omega))}}\right)

\leq\,2\left\{M^{2}e^{2|\omega|T}\|u_{0}\|^{2}_{{}_{\infty,\Omega}}+C^{\ast}_{1}\left(\|\tilde{u}\|_{{}_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))}}+\|f\|_{{}_{L^{\kappa_{1}}(0,T;L^{p}(\Omega))}}+\|g\|_{{}_{L^{\kappa_{2}}(0,T;L^{q}_{\mu}(\Gamma))}}\right)\right\}.

(4.61)

Furthermore, an application of Proposition
4
with Remark
6
and Hölder’s inequality gives that

\|\tilde{u}\|_{{}_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))}}\leq\,cT\left(\|u_{0}\|^{2}_{{}_{\infty,\Omega}}+\|f\|_{{}_{L^{\kappa_{1}}(0,T;L^{p}(\Omega))}}+\|g\|_{{}_{L^{\kappa_{2}}(0,T;L^{q}_{\mu}(\Gamma))}}\right),

(4.62)

for some constant
c>0
.
Combining (
4.61
) and (
4.62
) yield the inequality (
4.60
), as asserted.

\Box

Finally, we show that if problem (
1.8
) is local in time, then we obtain global boundedness in space.

Theorem 8
.

Suppose that conditions
(A3)’
and
(B4)’
hold.
Given
T_{2}>T_{1}>0
arbitrary fixed, let
\kappa_{1},\,\kappa_{2},\,p,\,q\in[2,\infty)
be as in Theorem
7
.
Given
u_{0}\in L^{2}(\Omega)
, let
f\in L^{\kappa_{1}}(0,T;L^{p}(\Omega))
and
g\in L^{\kappa_{2}}(0,T;L^{q}_{\mu}(\Gamma))
, and
let
u\in C(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(0,T;\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma))
be a weak solution of problem (
1.8
).
Then there exists a constant
C^{\ast}_{3}=C^{\ast}_{3}(T_{1},T_{2},N,d,\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},p,q,|\Omega|,\mu(\Gamma))>0
such that

|\|\mathbf{u}\||_{{}_{L^{\infty}(T_{1},T_{2};\mathbb{X\!}^{\,\infty}(\Omega;\Gamma))}}\,\leq\,C^{\ast}_{3}\left(\|u_{0}\|_{{}_{2,\Omega}}+\|f\|_{{}_{L^{\kappa_{1}}(0,T;L^{p}(\Omega))}}+\|g\|_{{}_{L^{\kappa_{2}}(0,T;L^{q}_{\mu}(\Gamma))}}\right).

(4.63)

Proof.

If
u\in C(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(0,T;\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma))
is a weak solution of (
1.8
), then by using the fact that
[T_{1},T_{2}]\subseteq\displaystyle\bigcup^{m}_{j=1}[T^{\ast}_{j}/2,T^{\ast}_{j}]

with
T^{\ast}_{1}<T^{\ast}_{2}<\ldots<T^{\ast}_{m}
(for some
m\in\mathbb{N\!}\,
), and
T^{\ast}_{1}=T_{1}
,
T^{\ast}_{m}=T_{2}
,
one can the full strength of Theorem
6
to get that

|\|\mathbf{u}\||_{{}_{L^{\infty}(T_{1},T_{2};\mathbb{X\!}^{\,\infty}(\Omega;\Gamma))}}\,\leq\,C^{\ast}_{1}\left(\|u\|_{{}_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))}}+\|f\|_{{}_{L^{\kappa_{1}}(0,T;L^{p}(\Omega))}}+\|g\|_{{}_{L^{\kappa_{2}}(0,T;L^{q}_{\mu}(\Gamma))}}\right).

(4.64)

Moreover, an application of Proposition
4
with Remark
6
and Hölder’s inequality gives that

\|u\|_{{}_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))}}\leq\,c^{\prime}\left(\|u_{0}\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega}}+\|f\|_{{}_{L^{\kappa_{1}}(0,T;L^{p}(\Omega))}}+\|g\|_{{}_{L^{\kappa_{2}}(0,T;L^{q}_{\mu}(\Gamma))}}\right).

(4.65)

for some constant
c^{\prime}>0
.
Combining (
4.64
) and (
4.65
) gives (
4.63
), as desired.

\Box

5.
Examples: domains

In the following section we provide multiple examples of bounded connected non-smooth and irregular domains. Most of the domains
will have the extension property, but at the end we will briefly discuss the situation when the domain fails to be an extension domain.

5.1.
(\epsilon,\delta)
-domains

Definition 8
.

A domain
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R\!}^{N}
is said to be an
(
\mathbf{\epsilon,\,\delta}
)
-
domain
,
if there exists
\delta\in(0,+\infty]
and there exists
\epsilon\in(0,1]
, such that for each two points
x,\,y\in\Omega

with
|x-y|\leq\delta
, there exists a continuous rectifiable curve
\gamma:[0,t]\rightarrow\Omega
such that
\gamma(0)=x
and

\gamma(t)=y
, with the following properties:

(a)

\ell(\{\gamma\})\leq\frac{1}{\epsilon}|x-y|
.

(b)

\textrm{dist}(z,\partial\Omega)\geq\,\epsilon\,\min\{|x-z|,|y-z|\},\,\,\,\textrm{for all}\,\,z\in\{\gamma\}
.

Also, an
(\epsilon,\,\infty)
-domain is called an
uniform domain
.

For this class of domains, it was established by Jones in a well-known result (see
[
54
, Theorem 1]
) that

(\epsilon,\delta)
-domains are extension domains for all
p\in[1,\infty]
. Since we are assuming that the domains are connected, then again
in
[
54
]
, it was shown that extension domains are equivalent to
(\epsilon,\delta)
-domains when
N=2
. For
N>2
however, there exist
extension domains who fail to be
(\epsilon,\delta)
-domains (see for instance
[
53
,
105
]
).
Furthermore, we recall that if
\Omega
is a bounded
(\epsilon,\delta)
-domain whose boundary is a

d
-set with respect to a measure
\mu
, then it was obtained in
[
57
]
that this condition is equivalent to say that
the
d
-dimensional Hausdorff measure
\mathcal{H}^{d}
is a
d
-Ahlfors measure on
\Gamma
.

Now we present several more concrete examples of
(\epsilon,\delta)
-domains.

Example 1
.

If
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R\!}^{N}
is a bounded Lipschitz domain,
then it is well known that
\Omega
is an extension domain, and the classical surface measure

\mathcal{H}^{N-1}
is an upper
(N-1)
-Ahlfors measure on
\Gamma
.

Example 2
.

Let
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R\!}^{\,2}
be the classical Koch snowflake domain (see the image below).
\lxSVG@sh@defs
\lxSVG@pos
\lxSVG@sh

Then it is well known that
\Omega
is an extension domain, and the
d
-dimensional Hausdorff measure

\mathcal{H}^{d}
restricted to
\Gamma:=\partial\Omega
is an upper
d
-Ahlfors measure on
\Gamma
, for
d:=\log(4)/\log(3)
(e.g.
[
98
]
).

Example 3
.

Motivated by some recent results in
[
43
]
, given
m>2
such that
m\not\equiv 0\,(\bmod\,\,3)
, we define the
Koch
\mathbf{m}
-Crystal

\Omega_{m}\subseteq\mathbb{R\!}^{\,3}
as the interior of the closed set enclosed by four congruent Koch
m
-Surfaces,
each pair of which intersect at precisely one edge (see Figure 1, and refer to
[
43
]
for more details of this construction).
We then define
\Gamma_{m}
as the boundary of
\Omega_{m}
.

Figure 1
.
Koch 4-Crystal and 5-Crystal, First and Second Iterations

Then it is shown in
[
43
]
that
\Omega_{m}
is an extension domain, and the
d
-dimensional Hausdorff measure

\mathcal{H}^{d}
restricted to
\Gamma:=\partial\Omega
is an upper
d
-Ahlfors measure on
\Gamma
, for
d:=\log(m^{2}+2)/\log(m)
.

5.2.
\delta
-Reifenberg flat domains

In this subsection we present a class of rough domains who, under certain conditions, fulfill the required properties required for the
validity of all the results in previous sections. We begin with the following definition, taken from
[
60
,
72
]
.

Definition 9
.

A domain
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R\!}^{N}
is said to be
(\delta,\rho_{0})
-Reifenberg flat
, if the following conditions hold:

(a)

For each
x_{0}\in\Gamma:=\partial\Omega
and
\rho\in(0,\rho_{0})
, there exits an hyperplane
\Upsilon
containing
x_{0}
, such that

d_{{}_{\mathcal{H}}}\left(\Gamma\cap B(x_{0},\rho),\,\Upsilon\cap B(x_{0},\rho)\right)\,\leq\,\delta\rho,

where
d_{{}_{\mathcal{H}}}(\cdot,\cdot)
denotes the Hausdorff distance function.

(b)

The set
\left\{x\in\Omega\cap B(x_{0},\rho)\mid d(x,\Gamma)\geq 2\delta\rho\right\}
is contained in one component of
\mathbb{R\!}^{N}\setminus\Upsilon
.

Furthermore, if in addition the boundary
\Gamma
is a
(N-1)
-set wit respect to the
(N-1)
-dimensional Hausdorff measure, then
\Omega

is called a
(\rho_{0},\delta)
-Reifenberg flat chord arc domain
.

The following key result was established by Lemenant, Milakis, and Spinolo
[
72
]
.

Theorem 9
.

(see
[
72
]
)  If
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R\!}^{N}
is a
(\delta,\rho_{0})
-Reifenberg flat domain for

\delta\leq 1/600
and
\rho_{0}>0
, then
\Omega
is an
(\epsilon,\,\delta^{\prime})
-domain for
\epsilon:=1/450
and
\delta^{\prime}:=\rho_{0}/7
.

As a direct consequence of the above theorem, one sees that if
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R\!}^{N}
is a
(\delta,r_{0})
-Reifenberg flat chord arc domain for

\delta\leq 1/600
, then the domain satisfy all the conditions in Assumption
1
.

5.3.
Ramified domains

We now consider a class of domains with ramified fractal-like boundaries. Such domains are of particular interest in applications to
bronchial trees (see for instance
[
3
,
6
,
87
]
among others).

In fact, we consider the following two particular domains
\Omega_{1}
and
\Omega_{2}
investigated in

[
3
,
4
,
5
,
6
]
(among others).
Let
\{F_{1},F_{2}\}
and
\{G_{1},G_{2}\}
denote two sets, each one consisting of a pair of similitudes on
\mathbb{R\!}^{\,2}
defined by

F_{i}(x_{1},x_{2}):=\left(\begin{array}[]{lcl}(-1)^{i}\displaystyle\frac{3}{2}+\displaystyle\frac{x_{1}}{2}\\
\\
\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,3+\displaystyle\frac{x_{2}}{2}\\
\end{array}\right)

and

G_{i}(x_{1},x_{2}):=\left(\begin{array}[]{lcl}(-1)^{i}\left[1-\displaystyle\frac{\tau}{\sqrt{2}}\right]+\displaystyle\frac{\tau}{\sqrt{2}}[x_{1}+(-1)^{i}x_{2}]\\
\\
1+\displaystyle\frac{\tau}{\sqrt{2}}+\displaystyle\frac{\tau}{\sqrt{2}}[x_{2}+(-1)^{i+1}x_{1}]\\
\end{array}\right)

Figure 2.
Figure above
: the domain
\Omega_{1}
generated by the maps
F_{i}
(taken from
[
3
]
);

Figure below
: the domain
\Omega_{2}
generated by the maps
G_{i}
when
\tau=\tau^{\ast}
(taken from
[
6
]
).

(
i\in\{1,2\}
) for
1/2\leq\tau<\tau^{\ast}:=0.593465
.
Let
\mathfrak{A}_{n}
be the set containing all the maps from

\{1,\ldots,n\}
to
\{1,2\}
. Then for
\sigma\in\mathfrak{A}_{n}
, we define the affine map

\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(H_{1},H_{2}):=H_{\sigma(1)}\circ\cdot\cdot\cdot\circ H_{\sigma(n)}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{(}\,H\,\,\textrm{stands as either}\,\,F,\,\,\textrm{or}\,\,G\,\textrm{)}.

Then, letting
V_{1}
be the interior of the set
([-1,1]\times[0,2])\cup([-2,2]\times[2,3])
, and
V_{2}
be the interior of the convex hull of the
points
P_{1}=(-1,0)
,
P_{2}=(1,0)
,
P_{3}=G_{1}(P_{1})
,
P_{4}=G_{2}(P_{2})
,
P_{5}=G_{1}(P_{2})
, and
P_{6}=G_{2}(P_{1})

we define

\Omega_{1}:=\textrm{Interior}\left(\overline{V_{1}}\cup\left(\displaystyle\bigcup^{\infty}_{n=1}\displaystyle\bigcup_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{A}_{n}}\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(F_{1},F_{2})(\overline{V_{1}})\right)\right),

and

\Omega_{2}:=\textrm{Interior}\left(\overline{V_{2}}\cup\left(\displaystyle\bigcup^{\infty}_{n=1}\displaystyle\bigcup_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{A}_{n}}\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(G_{1},G_{2})(\overline{V_{2}})\right)\right).

It follows that the ramified boundary
\Gamma^{\infty}_{i}
(
i\in\{1,2\}
) corresponds to the unique compact self-similar set in
\mathbb{R\!}^{\,2}
fulfilling

\Gamma^{\infty}_{1}=F_{1}(\Gamma^{\infty}_{1})\cup F_{2}(\Gamma^{\infty}_{1})\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\Gamma^{\infty}_{2}=G_{1}(\Gamma^{\infty}_{2})\cup G_{2}(\Gamma^{\infty}_{2}).

For more information and properties of these sets, refer to
[
3
,
4
,
5
,
6
]
(among others). In particular, one can summarize
several of the main properties in the following result.

Theorem 10
.

(see
[
4
,
3
,
5
,
6
]
) The following properties are valid for the sets

\Omega_{1}
and
\Omega_{2}
.

(a)

\Omega_{1}
and
\Omega_{2}
have the open set condition.

(b)

\Omega_{1}
is not an extension domain (
q=2
), but it is a
W^{1,q}
- extension domain for all
q\in[1,2)
.

(c)

\Omega_{2}
is an
(\epsilon,\delta)
-domain if
1/2\leq\tau<\tau^{\ast}
.

(d)

\Omega_{2}
is not an extension domain when
\tau=\tau^{\ast}
, but it is a
W^{1,q}
- extension domain for each
q\in[1,2)
.

(e)

The ramified boundary
\Gamma^{\infty}_{i}
(
1\leq i\leq 2
) is a
d_{i}
-set with respect to the self-similar
measure supported on
\Gamma^{\infty}_{i}
, where

d_{1}=1\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,d_{2}:=-\log(2)/\log(\tau).

For each
i\in\{1,2\}
, define now the function space

\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{2}(\Omega_{i};\Gamma^{\infty}_{i}):=\left\{w\in\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega_{i};\Gamma_{i})\mid w|_{{}_{\Gamma_{i}\setminus\Gamma^{\infty}_{i}}}=0\right\}.

Clearly
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{2}(\Omega_{i};\Gamma^{\infty}_{i})
is a closed subspace of

\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega_{i};\Gamma_{i})
. Then, combining this fact with a consequence of Theorem
10
, we see that the maps

\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{2}(\Omega_{i};\Gamma^{\infty}_{i})\hookrightarrow L^{r}(\Omega_{i})
and

\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{2}(\Omega_{i};\Gamma^{\infty}_{i})\hookrightarrow L^{s}_{\mu}(\Gamma^{\infty}_{i})
are linear and compact for each
r,\,s\in[1,\infty)
.

In this case, we then consider the solvability and regularity theory for the mixed-type inhomogeneous boundary elliptic problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{A}u\,=\,f(x)\indent\,\textrm{in}\,\,\Omega_{i}\\
\,\,\,u=0\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{in}\,\,\Gamma_{i}\setminus\Gamma^{\infty}_{i}\\
\mathcal{B}u\,=\,g(x)\indent\,\textrm{on}\,\,\Gamma^{\infty}_{i}\\
\end{array}\right.

(5.1)

and the corresponding inhomogeneous parabolic problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}u_{t}-\mathcal{A}u\,=\,f(t,x)\indent\,\textrm{in}\,\,(0,\infty)\times\Omega_{i}\\
\,\,\,u=0\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{in}\,\,(0,\infty)\times\Gamma_{i}\setminus\Gamma^{\infty}_{i}\\
\,\,\,\,\mathcal{B}u\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,=\,g(t,x)\indent\,\textrm{on}\,\,(0,\infty)\times\Gamma^{\infty}_{i}\\
\,u(0,x)=u_{0}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{in}\,\,\Omega_{i}\end{array}\right.

(5.2)

for
i\in\{1,2\}
. Then, one proceeds in the exact way as in the derivation of the general results,to achieve the following consequence.

Theorem 11
.

Given
i\in\{1,2\}
and
T>0
a fixed constant, let
f\in L^{2}(\Omega_{i})
,
g\in L^{2}_{\mu}(\Gamma^{\infty}_{i})
,
\tilde{f}\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega_{i}))
, and

\tilde{g}\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}_{\mu}(\Gamma^{\infty}_{i}))
. Then the following hold.

(a)

Problem (
1.5
) is uniquely solvable over
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{2}(\Omega_{i};\Gamma^{\infty}_{i})
.
Moreover, if
f\in L^{p}(\Omega_{i})
for
p>1
, and
g\in L^{q}_{\mu}(\Gamma^{\infty}_{i})
for
q>1
, then
u
is globally bounded with

|\|\mathbf{u}\||_{{}_{\infty}}\,\leq\,C\left(\|f\|_{{}_{p,\Omega_{i}}}+\|g\|_{{}_{q,\Gamma^{\infty}_{i}}}+\|u\|_{{}_{2,\Omega_{i}}}\right),

for some constant
C>0
. Furthermore, if any of the conditions in Remark
2
holds, then one has that

|\|\mathbf{u}\||_{{}_{\infty}}\,\leq\,C\left(\|f\|_{{}_{p,\Omega_{i}}}+\|g\|_{{}_{q,\Gamma^{\infty}_{i}}}\right).

(b)

Problem (
1.8
) is uniquely solvable over
C([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega_{i}))\cap L^{2}((0,T),\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{2}(\Omega_{i};\Gamma^{\infty}_{i}))
. Moreover,
given
\tilde{p},\,\tilde{q}\in[2,\infty)
fulfilling

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\kappa_{\tilde{p}}}+\displaystyle\frac{1}{\tilde{p}}<1,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\displaystyle\frac{1}{\kappa_{\tilde{q}}}+\displaystyle\frac{1}{\tilde{q}}<1,

if
\tilde{f}\in L^{\kappa_{\tilde{p}}}(0,T;L^{\tilde{p}}(\Omega_{i}))
,
\tilde{g}\in L^{\kappa_{\tilde{q}}}(0,T;L^{\tilde{q}}_{\mu}(\Gamma^{\infty}_{i}))
, and
u_{0}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega_{i})
, then

\|u\|_{{}_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{\infty}(\Omega_{i}))}}\,\leq\,C\left(\|u_{0}\|_{{}_{\infty,\Omega_{i}}}+\|\tilde{f}\|_{{}_{L^{\kappa_{\tilde{p}}}(0,T;L^{\tilde{p}}(\Omega_{i}))}}+\|\tilde{g}\|_{{}_{L^{\kappa_{\tilde{q}}}(0,T;L^{\tilde{q}}_{\mu}(\Gamma^{\infty}_{i}))}}\right),

for some constant
C>0
. Additionally, all the results in subsections
4.2
and
4.3
are valid for this given situation.

Recently, new fine global regularity results for the local version of problems (
5.1
) and (
5.2
) have been obtained in
[
87
]
.

5.4.
General bounded domains

To complete this section, let us consider the general case in which
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R\!}^{N}
is an arbitrary bounded domain.
For this case, we assume that
\Gamma:=\partial\Omega
has positive and finite
(N-1)
-dimensional Hausdorff measure (
0<\mathcal{H}^{N-1}(\Gamma)<\infty
),
and we assume in addition that
\mathcal{H}^{N-1}
is an admissible measure in the sense of Arendt and Warma
[
17
]
. Observe that this situation excludes
classes of domains such as the snowflake domain given in Example
2
(since the Koch curve has infinite
(N-1)
-Hausdorff measure), but may include other
general regions lacking the extension property, and such that their boundaries are not upper
(N-1)
-sets with respect to
\mathcal{H}^{N-1}
. For this case, it is
clear that Sobolev functions may lack a well-defined trace, and if such trace exists, it may not be
p
-integrable (
p\in[1,\infty)
). Consequently, following the approach by
Maz’ya
[
77
]
, we define the
Maz’ya’s space

W^{1}_{p}(\overline{\Omega})
as the abstract completion of the space

V^{1}_{p}(\overline{\Omega}):=\left\{u\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)\cap C_{c}(\overline{\Omega})\mid u|_{{}_{\Gamma}}\in L^{p}_{{}_{\mathcal{H}^{N-1}}}(\Gamma)\right\}

with respect to the norm

\|u\|_{{}_{W^{1}_{p}(\overline{\Omega})}}:=\left(\|\nabla u\|^{p}_{{}_{p,\Omega}}+\|u|_{{}_{\Gamma}}\|^{p}_{{}_{p,\Gamma}}\right)^{1/p}.

A key result by Maz’ya
[
77
]
establishes the existence of a constant
c>0
such that

\|u\|_{{}_{\frac{Np}{N-1}}}\,\leq\,c\,\|u\|_{{}_{W^{1}_{p}(\overline{\Omega})}}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{for all}\,\,\,u\in W^{1}_{p}(\overline{\Omega}),

(5.3)

and moreover the linear map
S:W^{1}_{p}(\overline{\Omega})\rightarrow L^{r}(\Omega)
is compact whenever
1\leq r<Np(N-1)^{-1}
.
Inequality (
5.3
) is optimal unless one assumes additional regularity properties over
\Omega
(see for instance
[
78
, Example 2.11]
).

To consider nonlocal-type boundary value problems with structure similar as in (
1.5
), we need to restrict the problem to a subspace of

W^{1}_{2}(\overline{\Omega})
. In fact, define the space

\mathbb{W}^{\,1}_{\,p}(\overline{\Omega}):=\left\{w\in W^{1}_{p}(\overline{\Omega})\cap\mathbb{B\!}^{\,p}_{\,\mathfrak{s}}(\Omega)\mid w|_{{}_{\Gamma}}\in\mathbb{B\!}^{\,p}_{{}_{\,1-\frac{N-d}{p}}}(\Gamma,\mathcal{H}^{N-1})\right\},

endowed with the norm

\|w\|_{{}_{\mathbb{W}^{\,1}_{\,p}(\overline{\Omega})}}:=\|w\|_{{}_{W^{1}_{p}(\overline{\Omega})}}+\mathcal{M}^{p}_{\mathfrak{s}}(w,\Omega)+\mathcal{N}^{p}_{{}_{1-\frac{N-d}{p}}}(w,\Gamma,\mathcal{H}^{N-1}),

(5.4)

where the expressions
\mathcal{M}^{p}_{\mathfrak{s}}(w,\Omega)
and
\mathcal{N}^{p}_{{}_{1-\frac{N-d}{p}}}(w,\Gamma,\mathcal{H}^{N-1})
are given by
(
2.2
) and (
2.3
), respectively. Then one clearly deduce that the inequality (
5.3
) remain valid if
one replaces the space
W^{1}_{2}(\overline{\Omega})
by the subspace
\mathbb{W}^{\,1}_{\,p}(\overline{\Omega})
. Furthermore, since inequality (
5.3
)
holds for
p=1
, proceeding as in Remark
2
(e), for each
\epsilon>0
, one gets the existence of a constant
c^{\prime}>0
such that

\|u\|^{2}_{{}_{\frac{2N}{N-1},\Omega}}\,\leq\,\epsilon\|\nabla u\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega}}+\displaystyle\frac{c^{\prime}}{\epsilon}\left(|\|\mathbf{u}\||^{2}_{{}_{2}}+[\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\mathfrak{s}}(u,\Omega)]^{2}+[\mathcal{N}^{2}_{{}_{1-\frac{N-d}{2}}}(u,\Gamma,\mathcal{H}^{N-1})]^{2}\right),

(5.5)

for each
u\in\mathbb{W}^{\,1}_{\,2}(\overline{\Omega})
.

We now turn the solvability (over arbitrary bounded domains) of the boundary value problems

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\,\,\,\,\,\mathcal{A}u\,=\,f(x)\indent\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{in}\,\,\Omega\\
(\mathcal{B}-\mathcal{L}_{\mu})u\,=\,0\indent\,\,\textrm{on}\,\,\Gamma\\
\end{array}\right.

(5.6)

and

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}u_{t}-\mathcal{A}u\,=\,f(t,x)\indent\,\textrm{in}\,\,(0,\times)\times\Omega\\
(\mathcal{B}-\mathcal{L}_{\mu})u\,=\,0\indent\,\textrm{on}\,\,(0,\infty)\times\Gamma\\
\,u(x,0)=u_{0}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{in}\,\,\Omega,\end{array}\right.

(5.7)

where we recall that the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\mu}
is defined in (
1.3
), and we are assuming the fulfillment of (
1.4
).
In this case, we assume that
\alpha_{ij}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
,
\hat{a}_{i}\in L^{r_{1}}(\Omega)
,
\check{a}_{i}\in L^{r_{2}}(\Omega)
(
1\leq i,\,j\leq N
),
\lambda\in L^{r_{3}}(\Omega)
, and
\beta\in L^{\infty}_{{}_{\mathcal{H}^{N-1}}}(\Gamma)
, where
r_{1},\,r_{2}\geq 2N
, and
r_{3}\geq N
. Then, by virtue of (
5.3
) and
(
5.5
), one proceeds in the same way as in the proofs of Theorem 3, Theorem 4, and Theorem 8, we can deduce the following result.

Theorem 12
.

Given
T_{2}>T_{1}>0
fixed constants, let
f\in L^{2}(\Omega)
and
\tilde{f}\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))
, and assume that
\beta\in L^{\infty}_{\mu}(\Gamma)
(
\mu(\cdot)=\mathcal{H}^{N-1}(\cdot)
) is such that
\textrm{ess}\displaystyle\inf_{\Gamma}[\beta]\geq C_{{}_{\Gamma}}
for a sufficient large positive constant
C_{{}_{\Gamma}}
. Then the following hold.

(a)

Problem (
5.6
) is uniquely solvable over
\mathbb{W}^{\,1}_{\,2}(\overline{\Omega})
.
Moreover, one gets that

|\|\mathbf{u}\||_{{}_{m_{1}(p),m_{2}(p)}}\,\leq\,C\left(\|f\|_{{}_{p,\Omega}}+\|u\|_{{}_{2,\Omega}}\right),

for some constant
C>0
, where

m_{1}(p):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\frac{Np}{N-p},\,\,\textrm{if}\,\,p<N,\\[4.30554pt]
r\in(2^{\ast}_{{}_{N}},\infty),\,\,\textrm{if}\,\,p=N,\\[4.30554pt]
\,\,\,\,\,\infty,\,\,\textrm{if}\,\,p>N,\end{array}\right.\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,m_{2}(p):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\frac{(N-1)p}{N-p},\,\,\textrm{if}\,\,p<N;\\[4.30554pt]
s\in(2,\infty),\,\,\textrm{if}\,\,p\geq N,\\[4.30554pt]
\end{array}\right.

(5.8)

(for the infinity norm, it is only known in the interior for this case).

(b)

Problem (
5.7
) is uniquely solvable over
C([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}((0,T),\mathbb{W}^{\,1}_{\,2}(\overline{\Omega}))
. Moreover,
given
\tilde{p}\in[2,\infty)
fulfilling

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\kappa_{\tilde{p}}}+\displaystyle\frac{N}{\tilde{p}}<1,

if
\tilde{f}\in L^{\kappa_{\tilde{p}}}(0,T;L^{\tilde{p}}(\Omega))
, and
u_{0}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
, then

\|u\|_{{}_{L^{\infty}(T_{1},T_{2};L^{\infty}(\Omega))}}\,\leq\,C\left(\|u\|_{{}_{2,\Omega}}+\|\tilde{f}\|_{{}_{L^{\kappa_{\tilde{p}}}(0,T;L^{\tilde{p}}(\Omega))}}\right),

for some constant
C>0
.

Theorem
12
(a) was established by Daners
[
34
]
for the local case with bounded measurable coefficients, and a nonlocal model
involving the boundary nonlocal boundary operator given in subsection
7.1
has been investigated in
[
96
,
100
]
. The homogeneous local parabolic problem
of type (
5.7
) with bounded coefficients has been investigated by Daners
[
33
,
34
]
. Therefore, Theorem
12
generalizes all
the regularity results in
[
33
,
34
,
96
,
100
]
to the case of unbounded interior coefficients. On the other hand, a priori estimates for the inhomogeneous parabolic problem over arbitrary domains are developed for the first time here.

Remark 7
.

In general domains, the embedding
\mathbb{W}^{\,1}_{\,2}(\overline{\Omega})\hookrightarrow L^{2}_{{}_{\mathcal{H}^{N-1}}}(\Gamma)
cannot be improved, and
consequently, although existence and uniqueness of a solution for problems (
5.6
) and (
5.7
) with inhomogeneous boundary conditions
can be achieved whenever
g\in L^{q}_{{}_{\mathcal{H}^{N-1}}}(\Gamma)
for
q\geq 2
, it is still an open problem to deduce
L^{\infty}
-estimates for weak solutions
of equations (
5.6
) and (
5.7
) with inhomogeneous boundary conditions, even if the boundary data
g
is assumed to be smooth.
If the map
\mathbb{W}^{\,1}_{\,2}(\overline{\Omega})\hookrightarrow L^{r}_{{}_{\mathcal{H}^{N-1}}}(\Gamma)
is continuous for some
r>2
, then one could obtain
a priori estimates for the case of inhomogeneous boundary conditions (under the suitable assumption on the boundary data
g
), but clearly there are multiple
domains with “bad” boundaries in which such embedding fails to be satisfied.

5.5.
Importance of boundary value problems over irregular domains

In the real world, many of the regions and surfaces where phenomena occur posses rough and irregular structure. In fact, when dealing with objects such as
clouds, trees, leaves, corals, snowflakes, blood vessels, and rocks (among many others), one cannot expect that such objects may have a structure compatible
with smooth manifolds (e.g.
[
75
]
). The interest in analysis and modeling of boundary value problems over domains with irregular boundaries such as
of fractal-like structure, began with the goal of understanding transmission problems, which, in electrostatics and magnetostatics, describe heat transfer
through a fractal-like interface (see for instance
[
66
,
67
]
and the references therein). Furthermore, the regions showcased in
subsection
5.3
have been used to simulate the diffusion of medical sprays in the bronchial tree (e.g.
[
1
,
2
,
3
,
4
,
5
,
6
,
7
,
38
,
87
]
). It follows that in order to get an accurate model of diffusion, inhomogeneous Neumann or Robin boundary value problems
need to be considered (Cf.
[
6
,
87
]
). Other applications include plant leaf identification
[
25
]
, oceanography
[
75
]
, and
probability theory
[
20
]
. Further applications can be found in
[
89
]
.

6.
Examples: Wentzell-type energy forms

In this section, we will present several Wentzell-type bilinear forms, which give rise to several generalized Wentzell
boundary value problems in which all the results in the previous sections hold. We will assume that all the conditions in
Assumption
1
are satisfied.

6.1.
Wentzell energy over compact Riemannian manifolds

Let us assume that the boundary
\Gamma
is a compact
(N-1)
-dimensional Riemannian manifold with Riemannian metric
\mathfrak{g}
,
for
N\geq 3
. Then, we consider

\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}}(u,v):=\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}\left(\displaystyle\sum^{N-1}_{{}^{i,j=1}}(\omega_{ij}(x)\partial_{x_{\tau_{j}}}u+\hat{b}_{i}(x)u)\partial_{x_{\tau_{i}}}v+\displaystyle\sum^{N-1}_{{}^{i=1}}\check{b}_{i}(x)\partial_{x_{\tau_{i}}}uv\right)\,d\mu(\mathfrak{g}),

(6.1)

for each
u,\,v\in D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}}):=H^{1}_{\mu(\mathfrak{g})}(\Gamma)
, where

\partial_{x_{\tau_{j}}}u
denotes the directional derivative of
u
along the tangential directions
\tau_{j}
at each point on
\Gamma

(
j\in\{1,\ldots,N-1\}
). Here
\omega_{ij}\in L^{\infty}_{\mu(\mathfrak{g})}(\Gamma)
,
\hat{b}_{i}\in L^{\hat{s}}_{\mu(\mathfrak{g})}(\Gamma)
,

\check{b}_{i}\in L^{\check{s}}_{\mu(\mathfrak{g})}(\Gamma)
(
i,\,j\in\{1,\ldots,N-1\}
), where

\hat{s},\,\check{s}>(N-1)\chi_{{}_{\{N>3\}}}+\chi_{{}_{N=3}}
. We also assume that
a strongly ellipticity property holds, in the sense that there exists a constant
\eta^{\prime}_{0}>
such that

\indent\indent\displaystyle\sum^{N-1}_{{}^{i,j=1}}\omega_{ij}(x)\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\,\geq\,\eta^{\prime}_{0}\,|\xi|^{2},\,\,\,\,\,\forall\,x\in\Gamma,\,\,\,\forall\,\xi=(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{{}_{N-1}})\in\mathbb{R\!}^{N-1}.

(6.2)

Observe that in general the form
\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}}(\cdot,\cdot)
is non-symmetric. In fact, it follows that the bilinear form (
3.4
)
has the same structure as the interior form given in (
3.3
). Then clearly conditions
(B1)
,

(B2)
, and
(B3)
, are fulfilled. Moreover, since the embedding
H^{1}_{\mu(\mathfrak{g})}(\Gamma)\hookrightarrow L^{\bar{s}}_{\mu(\mathfrak{g})}(\Gamma)

is compact whenever
1\leq\bar{s}<2(N-1)(N-3)^{-1}\chi_{{}_{\{N>3\}}}+\infty\chi_{{}_{\{N=3\}}}
, in the same way as in the derivation of Remark
2
(d),
we get that

\|u\|^{2}_{{}_{\bar{s},\Gamma}}\,\leq\,\epsilon\left\|\nabla_{{}_{\Gamma}}u\right\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Gamma}}+C_{\epsilon}\|u\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Gamma}}

for each
u\in H^{1}_{\mu(\mathfrak{g})}(\Gamma)
, for every
\epsilon>0
, and for some constant
C_{\epsilon}>0
. Consequently, proceeding in an analogous way
as in the proof of Proposition
1
, we deduce that the form
(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}},D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}}))
is continuous and weakly coercive.
Moreover, note that the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\mu}
abstractly given by (
1.3
) can be written explicitly by

\mathcal{L}_{\mu}u:=-\displaystyle\sum^{N-1}_{{}^{i,j=1}}\partial_{x_{\tau_{i}}}\left(\omega_{ij}(x)\partial_{x_{\tau_{j}}}u+\hat{b}_{i}(x)u\right)+\displaystyle\sum^{N-1}_{i=1}\check{b}_{i}(x)\partial_{x_{\tau_{i}}}u,

(6.3)

for each
u\in H^{1}_{\mu(\mathfrak{g})}(\Gamma)
. We also recall that in this case, the bound improvements given in Remark
3
hold, and in addition,
for this case we can assume that
\beta\in L^{s}_{\mu(\mathfrak{g})}(\Gamma)
for
s>\frac{N-1}{2}
.

A particular example of the above situation occurs when
\Omega
is at least a bounded Lipschitz domain (see Example
1
). In this case,
it is well-known that the boundary
\Gamma
is a compact Riemannian manifold, and
\mu(\mathfrak{g})=\mathcal{H}^{N-1}
, the

(N-1)
-dimensional Hausdorff measure restricted to
\Gamma
, which in this case coincide
\mathcal{H}^{N-1}
-a.e. with the usual
Surface measure on
\Gamma
. Classical Wentzell-type problems have been investigated mostly over Lipschitz domains, but a boundary value problem including a boundary
operator of type (
6.3
) is practically unknown in the literature. Recently in
[
9
,
10
]
, the authors considered a Wentzell-type
problem (also known as Venttsel’ problem) involving the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\mu}
given by (
6.3
) over a bounded domain of class
C^{1,1}
.
However, in their problem they assume that the coefficient
\omega_{ij}
is symmetric, and that
\hat{b}_{i}\equiv 0
. In our knowledge, these are the only
papers considering Wentzell boundary value problems whose structure are very similar to the one considered in this paper
(but less general in some sense than the one considered in (
6.3
)). A big generalization in
[
9
,
10
]
comes from the fact that
in their problems they are considering the principal matrix coefficients
a_{ij}
and
\omega_{ij}
to be VMO (which include some unbounded cases). We will not go into further details here.

6.2.
The Wentzell problem on the Koch snowflake domain

We denote by
|P-P_{0}|
the Euclidean distance in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and the Euclidean balls by
B(P_{0},r)=\{P\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:|P-P_{0}|<r\}
,
P_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{2},r>0
. By the snowflake
\Gamma:=\partial\Omega
we denote the union of three coplanar
Koch curves
K_{i}
(see Example
2
, and
[
42
]
).
We assume that the junction points
A_{1},A_{3},A_{5}
are the vertices of a regular triangle with unit side length, that is
|A_{1}-A_{3}|=|A_{1}-A_{5}|=|A_{3}-A_{5}|=1.

K_{1}
is the uniquely determined self-similar set with respect to a family
\Psi^{1}
of four suitable contractions
\psi_{1}^{(1)},...,\psi_{4}^{(1)}
, with respect to the same ratio
\frac{1}{3}
. Let
V_{0}^{(1)}:=\{A_{1},A_{3}\}
,
V_{j_{1}...j_{h}}^{(1)}:=\psi_{j_{1}}^{(1)}\circ...\circ\psi_{j_{h}}^{(1)}(V_{0}^{(1)})
and

V_{h}^{(1)}:=\bigcup\limits_{j_{1}...j_{h}=1}^{4}V_{j_{1}...j_{h}}^{(1)}
.

We set
V_{\star}^{(1)}:=\cup_{h\geq 0}V_{h}^{(1)}
. It holds that
K_{1}=\overline{V_{\star}^{(1)}}
.

In a similar way, it is possible to approximate
K_{2},K_{3}
by the sequences
(V_{h}^{(2)})_{h\geq 0}
,
(V_{h}^{(3)})_{h\geq 0}
, and denote their limits by
V_{\star}^{(2)},V_{\star}^{(3)}
. In order to approximate
\Gamma
, we define the increasing sequence of finite sets of points
\mathcal{V}_{h}:=\cup_{i=1}^{3}V_{h}^{(i)},h\geq 1
and
\mathcal{V}_{\star}:=\cup_{h\geq 1}\mathcal{V}_{h}
. It holds that
\mathcal{V}_{\star}=\cup_{i=1}^{3}V_{\star}^{(i)}
and
\Gamma=\overline{V_{\star}}
.

Moreover, on each
K_{i}
there exists a unique Borel probability measure
\mu_{i}
which is self-similar with respect to the family

\Psi^{(i)}
. Therefore, one can define, in a natural way, a finite Borel measure
\mu_{{}_{\Gamma}}
supported on
\Gamma
by

\mu_{{}_{\Gamma}}:=\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}+\mu_{3}.

(6.4)

The measure
\mu_{{}_{\Gamma}}
is a
d
-measure (see
[
57
]
), in the sense that there exist two positive constants
c_{1},c_{2}

c_{1}r^{d}\leq\mu_{{}_{\Gamma}}(B(P,r)\cap\Gamma)\leq c_{2}r^{d},\indent\textrm{for all}\,\,\,P\in\Gamma,

where
d=d_{f}=\frac{\log(4)}{\log(3)}.

In
[
44
]
the energy form
(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}},D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}}))
on the Koch snowflake has been constructed.

(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}},D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}}))
is a nonnegative energy functional in
L^{2}_{\mu}(\Gamma)
and the
following result holds.

Theorem 13
.

(see
[
26
]
) The following properties hold.

(1)

D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}})
is complete in the norm
\|u\|_{{}_{D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}})}}:=\|u\|_{{}_{2,\Gamma}}+\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}}(u,u)^{1/2}
.

(2)

D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}})
is dense in
L^{2}_{\mu}(\Gamma)
.

It follows that
D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}})
is injected in
L^{2}_{\mu}(\Gamma)
and it is a Hilbert space with
the scalar product associated to the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{D}(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}})}
. Then
we extend
\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}}
as usual on the completed space
D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}})
.
By
\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}}(\cdot,\cdot)
we denote the bilinear form defined on
D\left(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}}\right)\times D\left(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}}\right)
by polarization, i.e.

\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}}(u,v):=\frac{1}{2}\left(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}}[u+v]-\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}}[u]-\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}}[v]\right),\quad u,v\in D\left(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}}\right),

where
\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}}[w]:=\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}}(w,w)
. The form
\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}}(\cdot,\cdot)
is regular and strongly local.
Moreover, the functions in
D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}})
posses
continuous representatives, which are actually Hölder continuous
on
F
(as the next result shows).

Proposition 5
.

(see
[
68
, Proposition 2.4 and Corollary 3.3]
)
The space
D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}})
is continuously embedded into
\mathbb{B\!}^{\,2}_{\,\beta}(\Gamma,\mu)

and
C^{0,\beta}(\Gamma)
, respectively, where
C^{0,\beta}(\Gamma)
denotes the space of Hölder
continuous functions with exponent
\beta=\frac{\ln 4}{2\ln 3}=\frac{d_{f}}{2}
over
\Gamma
.

In the following we identify
u\in D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}})
with its continuous
representative, still denoted by
u
. By proceeding as in
[
44
]
, one can define on
\Gamma
a Lagrangian
(\mathcal{L},D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}}))

and an energy form
(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}},D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}}))
. More precisely,
\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}}(u,v)=\int_{\Gamma}d\mathcal{L}(u,v),

for every
u,\,v\in D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}})
where

D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}})=\{u\in C(\Gamma)\mid\,u|_{K_{i}}\in D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma_{i}}}),\,\,\,\textrm{for}\,\,\,i\in\{1,2,3\}\}.

In particular it holds that

\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}}[u]=\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{3}\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma_{i}}}i[u|_{K_{i}}].

(6.5)

Moreover, since the form
(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}},D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}}))
is a regular Dirichlet form on
L^{2}_{\mu}(\Gamma)
with
domain
D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}})
dense in
L^{2}_{\mu}(\Gamma)
,
by
[
59
, Chap. 6, Theorem 2.1]
, there exists an unique self-adjoint, non-positive operator on
L^{2}_{\mu}(\Gamma)
, which we denote by

\mathcal{L}_{\mu}
, with domain
D(\mathcal{L}_{\mu})\subseteq D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}})
, such that

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}d\mathcal{L}(u,v)=\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}}(u,v)=-\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}(\mathcal{L}_{\mu}u)v\,d\mu,\indent\textrm{for all}\,\,\,u\in D(\mathcal{L}_{\mu}),\,\,v\in D(\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}}).

(6.6)

From all the above constructions and known properties, one sees that
the form in (
3.4
) is symmetric and clearly satisfies
(B1)
and
(B2)
. Furthermore,
since
\mathcal{E}[u]
is strongly local, one has
(B3)
and a straightforward verification shows that conditions
(B4)

and
(B4)’
are fulfilled for
c^{\star}_{1}=1
, and
c^{\star}_{2}=c^{\star}_{3}=0
.

6.3.
Applications of Wentzell boundary value problems

There is a vast number of applications involving differential equations with Wentzell-type boundary conditions.
One can name few applications, such as in phase-transition phenomena,
fluid diffusion, heat flow subject to nonlinear cooling on the boundary, suspended transport energy,
fermentation, population dynamics, and climatology, among many others (see for example
[
18
,
39
,
41
,
85
]
).
Furthermore, Wentzell problems over fractal-like domains is valuable due to the fact that several natural and industrial
processes lead to the formation of rough surfaces or occur across them. Fractal boundaries and fractal layers may be of great interest for
those applications in which the surface effects are enhanced with respect to the surrounding volume
(see
[
27
,
28
,
29
]
for details and motivations).

7.
Examples: nonlocal operators

In this part we give concrete examples of nonlocal operators
\mathcal{J}_{{}_{\Omega}}
and
\Theta_{{}_{\Gamma}}
. Unless specified,
we will assume all the conditions of Assumption
1
.

7.1.
Besov-type nonlocal operators

We begin by considering the classical Besov-type operators, which are clear examples of nonlocal maps. These play a crucial role
in fractional differential equations, and thus could bring some sort of “fractional flavor” into the boundary value problems
(
1.5
).

To begin, the interior and boundary Besov-type operators are defined by the maps
\mathcal{J}_{{}_{\Omega}}:H^{\mathfrak{s}/2}(\Omega)\rightarrow H^{-\mathfrak{s}/2}(\Omega)
and
\Theta_{{}_{\Gamma}}:H^{r_{d}/2}_{\mu}(\Gamma)\rightarrow H^{-r_{d}/2}_{\mu}(\Gamma)
, explicitly given by

(\mathcal{J}_{{}_{\Omega}}u)v:=\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\int_{\Omega}\mathfrak{a}(x,y)\frac{(u(x)-u(y))(v(x)-v(y))}{|x-y|^{2\mathfrak{s}+N}}\,dxdy,

(7.1)

and

(\Theta_{{}_{\Gamma}}u)v:=\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}\int_{\Gamma}\mathfrak{b}(x,y)\frac{(u(x)-u(y))(v(x)-v(y))}{|x-y|^{2+2d-N}}\,d\mu_{x}d\mu_{y},

(7.2)

for
\mathfrak{s}\in(0,1)
, where
\mathfrak{a}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega\times\Omega,dx\oplus dy)
and
\mathfrak{b}\in L^{\infty}(\Gamma\times\Gamma,d\mu_{x}\oplus d\mu_{y})

are both nonnegative almost everywhere in their respective domains. Then in this case
the nonlocal form
K(\cdot,\cdot)
given by (
3.5
) is explicitly written as

K(u,v):=\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\int_{\Omega}\mathfrak{a}\frac{(u(x)-u(y))(v(x)-v(y))}{|x-y|^{2s+N}}\,dxdy+\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}\int_{\Gamma}\mathfrak{b}\frac{(u(x)-u(y))(v(x)-v(y))}{|x-y|^{2+2d-N}}\,d\mu_{x}d\mu_{y}.

(7.3)

Then a straightforward calculation shows that the conditions
(A1)
,
(A2)
,
(A3)
, and
(A3)’
are valid for this case.

7.2.
Dirichlet-to-Neumann map

We now consider a generalized notion of the classical Dirichlet-to-Neumann map, which is nonlocal
boundary operator. To begin, given
\gamma\in L^{\infty}(\Gamma)
with
\displaystyle{\textrm{ess}}\inf_{{}^{x\in\partial\Omega}}\gamma(x)\,\geq\,\gamma_{0}
for some constant
\gamma_{0}>0
, let
\mathfrak{w}
be the solution of the Dirichlet problem

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}-\textrm{div}(\gamma\nabla\mathfrak{w})\,=\,0\indent\,\textrm{in}\,\,\Omega\\
\,\,\,\mathfrak{w}|_{{}_{\Gamma}}\,=\,w\indent\indent\indent\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{on}\,\Gamma,\\
\end{array}\right.

(7.4)

for
w\in H^{r_{d}/2}_{\mu}(\Gamma)
given. Then we define the
Dirichlet-to-Neumann map

\mathcal{J}_{{}_{\Gamma}}:H^{r_{d}/2}_{\mu}(\Gamma)\rightarrow H^{-r_{d}/2}_{\mu}(\Gamma)
by

\mathcal{J}_{{}_{\Gamma}}w:=\gamma\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathfrak{w}}{\partial\nu_{\mu}},

(7.5)

where the normal derivative here is interpreted in the generalized sense (see Definition
1
or
[
97
, Definition 1.1]
).
For properties of this nonlocal map on sufficiently smooth domains (that is, for at least bounded Lipschitz domains),
one can refer to
[
13
,
55
,
56
,
91
]
(and the references therein). On rough non-smooth domain, one can refer
to the work by Arendt and ter Elst
[
15
]
.

Before proceeding with the verification of the properties of this nonlocal operator, we first state a
general result due to Arendt and Elst (e.g.
[
14
, Theorem 3.2]
), which will be needed for the next important result.

Theorem 14
.

(see
[
14
]
)  Let
(a,D(a))
be a sesquilinear form over a Hilbert space
H
, and let
j:D(a)\rightarrow H
be a linear map.
If
a
is
j
-sectorial (e.g.
[
14
, section 3, pag. 8]
) and
j(D(a))
is dense in
H
, then the following conditions hold:

(a)

There exists an operator
A
in
H
such that for all
x,\,y\in H
, one has
x\in D(A)
and
Ax=y
if and only if
there exists a sequence
\{u_{n}\}\subseteq D(a)
such that
\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}j(u_{n})=x
weakly in
H
,

\displaystyle\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N\!}}\textrm{Re}\{a(u_{n},u_{n})\}<\infty
, and
\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}a(u_{n},v)=\langle y,j(v)\rangle_{H}

for all
v\in D(a)
.

(b)

The operator
A
of Statement (a) is
m
-sectorial.

(c)

Given
x,\,y\in H
, one has
x\in D(a)
and
Ax=y
if and only if there exists a Cauchy sequence
\{u_{n}\}\subseteq D(a)

such that
\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}j(u_{n})=x
in H and

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}a(u_{n},v)=\langle y,j(v)\rangle_{H}
for all
v\in D(a)
.

Next, we establish the following
result, which is a sort of generalization (in the sense of more general measures) of
[
15
, Theorem 3.3]
.

Theorem 15
.

The operator
\mathcal{J}_{{}_{\Gamma}}
over
L^{2}_{\mu}(\Gamma)
exists, such that the following conditions hold: given
\varphi,\,\psi\in L^{2}_{\mu}(\Gamma)
,
one has
\varphi\in D(\mathcal{J}_{{}_{\Gamma}})
and
\mathcal{J}_{{}_{\Gamma}}\varphi=\psi
if and only if there exists
u\in H^{1}(\Omega)
satisfying

(a)

\Delta u=0
;

(b)

\varphi
is the trace of
u
;

(c)

\gamma\displaystyle\frac{\partial u}{\partial\nu_{\mu}}\in L^{2}_{\mu}(\Gamma)
and

\gamma\displaystyle\frac{\partial u}{\partial\nu_{\mu}}=\psi
.

Proof.

The proof runs as in
[
15
, proof of Theorem 3.3]
with minor changes, but for the sake of completeness, we spell the details below.
Given
H:=L^{2}_{\mu}(\Gamma)
, consider the bilinear form
(a,D(a))
given by:
D(a):=H^{1}(\Omega)\cap C(\overline{\Omega})
, and

a(v,w):=\gamma\int_{\Omega}\nabla v\nabla w\,dx
. Sea
j:D(a)\rightarrow L^{2}_{\mu}(\Gamma)
, defined by
j(u):=u_{{}_{\Gamma}}
.
Then
j(D(a))
is dense in
L^{2}_{\mu}(\Gamma)
, and
a(\cdot,\cdot)
is sectorial (e.g.
[
14
, Lemma 3.1]
).
Thus, let
A
be the operator associated with
(a,j)
in the sense of Theorem
14
. We show that
A
possesses the properties
of
\mathcal{J}_{{}_{\Gamma}}
. Indeed, let
\varphi,\,\psi\in L^{2}_{\mu}(\Gamma)
, and assume first that
\varphi\in D(A)
and
A\varphi=\psi
.
Then one can find a sequence
\{u_{n}\}\subseteq D(a)
such that

\displaystyle\lim_{n,\,m\rightarrow\infty}\|\nabla(u_{n}-u_{m})\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega}}=0,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}u_{n}|_{{}_{\Gamma}}=\varphi\,\,\,\textrm{in}\,\,L^{2}_{\mu}(\Gamma),

(7.6)

and

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\gamma\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\nabla u_{n}\nabla v\,dx=\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}\psi v\,d\mu,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{for every}\,\,v\in D(a).

(7.7)

Recalling Remark
2
(b), one sees that
\{u_{n}\}\subseteq H^{1}(\Omega)
is a Cauchy sequence, and thus

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}u_{n}=u
in
H^{1}(\Omega)
. Moreover, by (
7.7
) we see that

\gamma\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\nabla u\nabla v\,dx=\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}\psi v\,d\mu,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{for all}\,\,v\in D(a).

(7.8)

Taking
v\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega)
in (
7.8
), we see that
\Delta u=0
, and consequently,

\gamma\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}(\Delta u)v\,dx+\gamma\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\nabla u\nabla v\,dx=\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}\psi v\,d\mu,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{for every}\,\,v\in D(a).

(7.9)

Integrating by parts (in the generalized way), we find that
\gamma\displaystyle\frac{\partial u}{\partial\nu_{\mu}}\in L^{2}_{\mu}(\Gamma)
and

\gamma\displaystyle\frac{\partial u}{\partial\nu_{\mu}}=\psi
, as desired.

Conversely, suppose that there exists
u\in H^{1}(\Omega)
fulfilling the conditions in (a), (b), (c) of the theorem.
Then by density, there exists
\{u_{n}\}\subseteq D(a)
such that
\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}u_{n}=u
in
H^{1}(\Omega)
and

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}u_{n}|_{{}_{\Gamma}}=\varphi
in
L^{2}_{\mu}(\Gamma)
. Thus, we have that

\displaystyle\lim_{n,\,m\rightarrow\infty}a(u_{n}-u_{m},u_{n}-u_{m})=0
, and using the fact that
\Delta u=0
together with the definition of

\displaystyle\frac{\partial u}{\partial\nu_{\mu}}
, we find that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}a(u_{n},v)=\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\gamma\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\nabla u_{n}\nabla v\,dx=\gamma\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}(\Delta u)v\,dx+\gamma\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\nabla u\nabla v\,dx=\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}\psi v\,d\mu,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{for all}\,\,v\in D(a).

(7.10)

Equality (
7.10
) shows that
\varphi\in D(A)
and
A\varphi=\psi
. In particular, this shows that the operator with the properties
of
\mathcal{J}_{{}_{\Gamma}}
is well defined, and equals
A
. This completes the proof of the theorem.

\Box

Next, for
u,\,v\in\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)
, we define concretely the nonlocal form
K(\cdot,\cdot)
by

K(u,v):=\langle\mathcal{J}_{{}_{\Gamma}}u,v\rangle=\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}(\mathcal{J}_{{}_{\Gamma}}u)v\,d\mu,

(7.11)

for
\mathcal{J}_{{}_{\Gamma}}
given by (
7.5
). Notice that we are assuming that
\mathcal{J}_{{}_{\Gamma}}u\in L^{2}_{\mu}(\Gamma)
in the weak sense
(e.g. Theorem
15
and Definition
6
(c)). Then
K(\cdot,\cdot)
is symmetric and positive (e.g.
[
56
, Theorem 4.1]
).
We now show the validity of
(A1)
for this case. In fact,
given
u\in\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)
, let
\mathfrak{u}
be the solution of (
7.4
) for

g\equiv u
. Then we let
v_{{}_{m}}
be defined by (
3.29
), and consider the solution
\mathfrak{v}_{{}_{m}}
of problem
(
7.4
) for
g\equiv v_{{}_{m}}
. Then one has

\mathfrak{v}_{{}_{m}}|_{{}_{\Gamma}}=v_{{}_{m}}|_{{}_{\Gamma}}=\psi_{{}_{k-1,m}}(u)|_{{}_{\Gamma}}=\psi_{{}_{k-1,m}}(\mathfrak{u})|_{{}_{\Gamma}}\,\,\,\mu\textrm{-a.e. on}\,\,\Gamma,

where we recall the definition of the function
\psi_{{}_{t,m}}(\cdot)
given in (
3.28
).
Then, in views of (
7.5
) and (
7.11
), we get the following calculation.

K(u,v_{{}_{m}})=\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}\gamma v_{{}_{m}}\frac{\partial\mathfrak{u}}{\partial\nu_{\mu}}\,d\mu=\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}\gamma\psi_{{}_{k-1,m}}(\mathfrak{u})\frac{\partial\mathfrak{u}}{\partial\nu_{\mu}}\,d\mu=\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\gamma\nabla\psi_{{}_{k-1,m}}(\mathfrak{u})\nabla\mathfrak{u}\,dx

\indent\indent\indent\indent\,\geq\,m\displaystyle\int_{\Omega\cap\{\mathfrak{u}\geq m\}}|\nabla\mathfrak{u}|^{2}\,dx+(k-1)\displaystyle\int_{\Omega\cap\{\mathfrak{u}<m\}}|\mathfrak{u}|^{k-2}|\nabla\mathfrak{u}|^{2}\,dx\,\geq\,0

(recall that
k\geq 2
). This shows that
(A1)
holds for
\eta_{0}=0
. Moreover, a calculation similar as the one carried out in

[
13
, Theorem 4.1]
yields that
K(u^{+},u^{-})\leq 0
, establishing
(A2)
.

To establish the validity of
(A3)
, again given
u\in\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)
, let
\mathfrak{u}
be the solution of (
7.4
) for

g\equiv u|_{{}_{\Gamma}}
. Then one has that
u_{k}|_{{}_{\Gamma}}=(\mathfrak{u}|_{{}_{\Gamma}}-k)^{+}:=\mathfrak{u}_{k}|_{{}_{\Gamma}}
, and consequently

K(u,u_{k})=\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}\gamma u_{k}\frac{\partial\mathfrak{u}}{\partial\nu_{\mu}}\,d\mu=\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}\gamma\mathfrak{u}_{k}\frac{\partial\mathfrak{u}}{\partial\nu_{\mu}}\,d\mu=\displaystyle\int_{\widehat{\Omega}_{k}}\gamma\nabla\mathfrak{u}\nabla\mathfrak{u}\,dx\,\geq\,0,

where
\widehat{\Omega}_{k}:=\{x\in\Omega\mid\mathfrak{u}(x)\geq k\}
. We have shown the fulfillment of property
(A3)
. Analogously, one easily sees that
condition
(A3)’
is valid, and therefore all the results on this paper hold for the Dirichlet-to-Neumann map given by (
7.5
).

7.3.
Further remarks and results for nonlocal maps

Remark 8
.

Another class of nonlocal (boundary operators) has been investigated by Gesztezy and Mitrea
[
47
]
.
However, it may not be possible to show the fulfillment of the conditions
(A1)
and
(A2)
without giving more concrete
assumptions to the nonlocal operator presented in
[
47
]
. On the other hand, it may open the door to consider more classes of
nonlocal maps, and applications. We will no go further here.

We now establish a relationship between boundary value problems with different nonlocal maps.

Theorem 16
.

Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{{}_{\Omega}}:H^{s/2}(\Omega)\rightarrow H^{-s/2}(\Omega)
and

\widetilde{\Theta}_{{}_{\Gamma}}:H^{r_{d}/2}_{\mu}(\Gamma)\rightarrow H^{-r_{d}/2}_{\mu}(\Gamma)
be linear bounded (nonlocal)
operators, such that
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{{}_{\Omega}}1=\widetilde{\Theta}_{{}_{\Gamma}}1=0
,

\langle\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{{}_{\Omega}}u,1\rangle_{s/2}=0=\langle\widetilde{\Theta}_{{}_{\Gamma}}v,1\rangle_{r_{d}/2}
,
\langle\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{{}_{\Omega}}u,u\rangle_{s/2}\geq 0
,
and
\langle\widetilde{\Theta}_{{}_{\Gamma}}v,v\rangle_{r_{d}/2}\geq 0
,
for each
u\in H^{s/2}(\Omega)
and
v\in H^{r_{d}/2}_{\mu}(\Gamma)
. Given
u\in\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)
a weak solution of (
1.5
),
let
\tilde{u}\in\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)
be a weak solution of (
1.5
) related to the maps
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{{}_{\Omega}}

and
\widetilde{\Theta}_{{}_{\Gamma}}
. Then there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\|u-\tilde{u}\|_{{}_{\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)}}\,\leq\,C\,\left(\|\mathcal{J}_{{}_{\Omega}}u-\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{{}_{\Omega}}u\|_{{}_{H^{-s/2}(\Omega)}}+\|\Theta_{{}_{\Gamma}}u-\widetilde{\Theta}_{{}_{\Gamma}}u\|_{{}_{H^{-r_{d}/2}_{\mu}(\Gamma)}}+\|u-\tilde{u}\|_{{}_{2,\Omega}}\right).

(7.12)

Moreover, if any of the conditions in Remark
4
hold, then

\|u-\tilde{u}\|_{{}_{\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)}}\,\leq\,C^{\prime}\,\left(\|\mathcal{J}_{{}_{\Omega}}u-\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{{}_{\Omega}}u\|_{{}_{H^{-s/2}(\Omega)}}+\|\Theta_{{}_{\Gamma}}u-\widetilde{\Theta}_{{}_{\Gamma}}u\|_{{}_{H^{-r_{d}/2}_{\mu}(\Gamma)}}\right),

(7.13)

for some constant
C^{\prime}>0
.

Proof.

Clearly one has
\mathcal{E}_{\mu}(u-\tilde{u},\varphi)=0
for all
\varphi\in\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)

(recall the definition of
\mathcal{E}_{\mu}(\cdot,\cdot)
given in (
3.1
)). Moreover, since the (nonlocal) maps are linear, we see that

\mathcal{J}_{{}_{\Omega}}u-\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{{}_{\Omega}}\tilde{u}=(\mathcal{J}_{{}_{\Omega}}u-\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{{}_{\Omega}}u)+\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{{}_{\Omega}}(u-\tilde{u});

the same property holds for
\Theta_{{}_{\Gamma}}
and
\widetilde{\Theta}_{{}_{\Gamma}}
.
Using these facts, rearranging, applying Young’s inequality, using the positivity of both
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{{}_{\Omega}},\,\widetilde{\Theta}_{{}_{\Gamma}}
, applying
[
47
, Lemma 4.2 and 4.3]
to the
L^{2}
-boundary terms,
taking into account (
1.4
), and using (
2.8
) and (
2.9
), by selecting
\varphi:=u-\tilde{u}
one has

\min\{c_{0},1\}\|u-\tilde{u}\|^{2}_{{}_{\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)}}

\leq\,-\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left(\displaystyle\sum^{N}_{{}^{j=1}}\hat{a}_{j}(x)(u-\tilde{u})\partial_{j}(u-\tilde{u})+\displaystyle\sum^{N}_{{}^{i=1}}\check{a}_{i}(x)\partial_{i}(u-\tilde{u})(u-\tilde{u})+\lambda(x)|u-\tilde{u}|^{2}\right)\,dx+

-\Lambda_{{}_{\Gamma}}(u-\tilde{u},u-\tilde{u})+c\|u-\tilde{u}\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Gamma}}-\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}\beta(x)|u-\tilde{u}|^{2}\,d\mu+\indent\indent\indent

-\left(\left\langle\mathcal{J}_{{}_{\Omega}}u-\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{{}_{\Omega}}u,u-\tilde{u}\right\rangle_{s/2}+\left\langle\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{{}_{\Omega}}(u-\tilde{u}),u-\tilde{u}\right\rangle_{s/2}+\left\langle\Theta_{{}_{\Gamma}}u-\widetilde{\Theta}_{{}_{\Gamma}}u,u-\tilde{u}\right\rangle_{r_{d}/2}\right)+

\indent\indent\indent-\left\langle\widetilde{\Theta}_{{}_{\Gamma}}(u-\tilde{u}),u-\tilde{u}\right\rangle_{r_{d}/2}+\min\{c_{0},1\}\left\|\textbf{u}-\tilde{\textbf{u}}\right\|^{2}_{{}_{\mathbb{X\!}^{\,2}(\Omega;\Gamma)}}

\leq\,\epsilon\left\|\nabla(u-\tilde{u})\right\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega}}+C^{\ast}_{\epsilon}(\delta^{\ast}+c+\min\{c_{0},1\})\|u-\tilde{u}\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega}}+\indent\indent\indent\indent

\indent+\|\mathcal{J}_{{}_{\Omega}}u-\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{{}_{\Omega}}u\|_{{}_{H^{-s/2}(\Omega)}}\|u-\tilde{u}\|_{{}_{H^{s/2}(\Omega)}}+\|\Theta_{{}_{\Gamma}}u-\widetilde{\Theta}_{{}_{\Gamma}}u\|_{{}_{H^{-r_{d}/2}_{\mu}(\Gamma)}}\|u-\tilde{u}\|_{{}_{H^{r_{d}/2}_{\mu}(\Gamma)}}

\leq\,3\epsilon\left\|\nabla(u-\tilde{u})\right\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega}}+\{C^{\ast}_{\epsilon}(\delta^{\ast}+c+\min\{c_{0},1\})+C^{\prime}_{\epsilon}\}\|u-\tilde{u}\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega}}+

\indent\indent\indent\indent\indent\indent+\|\mathcal{J}_{{}_{\Omega}}u-\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{{}_{\Omega}}u\|^{2}_{{}_{H^{-s/2}(\Omega)}}+\|\Theta_{{}_{\Gamma}}u-\widetilde{\Theta}_{{}_{\Gamma}}u\|^{2}_{{}_{H^{-r_{d}/2}_{\mu}(\Gamma)}},

for all
\epsilon>0
, for some positive constants
c,\,C^{\ast}_{\epsilon},\,C^{\prime}_{\epsilon}
, and where the constant
\delta^{\ast}

is given by (
3.54
). By selecting
\epsilon>0
suitably, from the above calculation we deduce the validity of (
7.12
).
Furthermore, if any of the conditions in Remark
4
occur, then one gets immediately from the above estimates that
(
7.13
) is valid, completing the proof.

\Box

To conclude this section, we will establish
L^{\infty}
-type a priori estimates for elliptic and parabolic problems of type (
1.5
) and
(
1.8
), respectively, but now for the case where we have nonlocal interior and boundary data. To be more precise, consider the solvability of the
problems

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{A}u\,=\,\mathcal{J}^{{}_{B}}_{{}_{\Omega}}f(x)\indent\,\textrm{in}\,\,\Omega,\\
\mathcal{B}u\,=\,\Theta^{{}_{B}}_{{}_{\Gamma}}g(x)\indent\,\textrm{on}\,\,\Gamma,\\
\end{array}\right.

(7.14)

and

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}u_{t}-\mathcal{A}u\,=\,\mathcal{J}^{{}_{B}}_{{}_{\Omega}}\tilde{f}(t,x)\indent\,\textrm{in}\,\,(0,\infty)\times\Omega,\\
\,\,\,\,\mathcal{B}u\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,=\,\Theta^{{}_{B}}_{{}_{\Gamma}}\tilde{g}(t,x)\indent\,\textrm{on}\,\,(0,\infty)\times\Gamma,\\
\,u(0,x)=u_{0}(x)\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{in}\,\,\Omega,\end{array}\right.

(7.15)

where
\mathcal{J}^{{}_{B}}_{{}_{\Omega}}
and
\Theta^{{}_{B}}_{{}_{\Omega}}
denote the nonlocal operators defined by (
7.1
) and
(
7.2
) for
\mathfrak{a}=\mathfrak{b}=1
, respectively, and where in the denominator
N
and
2d-N
are replaced by
2N/\tilde{p}
and
(4d-2N)/\tilde{q}
, respectively (for corresponding values of
\tilde{p},\,\tilde{q}\in[2,\infty)
). Then our main result regarding this situation is the following.

Theorem 17
.

Given
T>0
a fixed constant, let
f\in H^{\mathfrak{s}/2}(\Omega)
,
g\in H^{r_{d}/2}_{\mu}(\Gamma)
,
\tilde{f}\in L^{2}(0,T;H^{\mathfrak{s}/2}(\Omega))
, and

\tilde{g}\in L^{2}(0,T;H^{r_{d}/2}_{\mu}(\Gamma))
. Write
\eta_{\tilde{q}}:=1-(N-d)/\tilde{q}
. Then the following hold.

(a)

Problem (
7.14
) is uniquely solvable over
\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma)
. Moreover, if
f\in\mathbb{B\!}^{\,\tilde{p}}_{\,\mathfrak{s}}(\Omega)

for
\tilde{p}>N
, and
g\in\mathbb{B\!}^{\,\tilde{q}}_{{}_{\,\eta_{\tilde{q}}}}(\Gamma,\mu)
for
\tilde{q}>2d(2+d-N)^{-1}
, then
u
is globally bounded with

|\|\mathbf{u}\||_{{}_{\infty}}\,\leq\,C\left(\mathcal{M}^{\tilde{p}}_{\mathfrak{s}}(f,\Omega)+\mathcal{N}^{\tilde{q}}_{\eta_{\tilde{q}}}(g,\Gamma,\mu)+\|u\|_{{}_{2,\Omega}}\right),

for some constant
C>0
, where we recall that
\mathcal{M}^{\tilde{p}}_{\mathfrak{s}}(f,\Omega)
and
\mathcal{N}^{\tilde{q}}_{\eta_{\tilde{q}}}(g,\Gamma,\mu)
are defined in
(
2.2
) and (
2.3
), respectively. Furthermore, if any of the conditions in Remark
2
holds, then one has that

|\|\mathbf{u}\||_{{}_{\infty}}\,\leq\,C\left(\mathcal{M}^{\tilde{p}}_{\mathfrak{s}}(f,\Omega)+\mathcal{N}^{\tilde{q}}_{\eta_{\tilde{q}}}(g,\Gamma,\mu)\right).

(b)

Given
\tilde{p},\,\tilde{q}\in[2,\infty)
fulfilling

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\kappa_{\tilde{p}}}+\displaystyle\frac{N}{\tilde{p}}<1,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\displaystyle\frac{1}{\kappa_{\tilde{q}}}+\displaystyle\frac{d}{\tilde{q}(2+d-N)}<\frac{1}{2},

if
\tilde{f}\in L^{\kappa_{\tilde{p}}}(0,T;\mathbb{B\!}^{\,\tilde{p}}_{\,\mathfrak{s}}(\Omega))
,
\tilde{g}\in L^{\kappa_{\tilde{q}}}(0,T;\mathbb{B\!}^{\,\tilde{q}}_{{}_{\,\eta_{\tilde{q}}}}(\Gamma,\mu))
,
u_{0}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
, and if
u\in C([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}((0,T),\mathcal{W}_{2}(\Omega;\Gamma))
is a weak solution to problem (
7.15
), then

\|u\|_{{}_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{\infty}(\Omega))}}\,\leq\,C\left(\|u_{0}\|_{{}_{\infty,\Omega}}+\left\{\displaystyle\int^{T}_{0}\left[\mathcal{M}^{\tilde{p}}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\tilde{f}(t),\Omega)\right]^{\kappa_{\tilde{p}}}dt\right\}^{1/\kappa_{\tilde{p}}}+\left\{\displaystyle\int^{T}_{0}\left[\mathcal{N}^{\tilde{q}}_{\eta_{\tilde{q}}}(\tilde{g}(t),\Gamma,\mu)\right]^{\kappa_{\tilde{q}}}dt\right\}^{1/\kappa_{\tilde{q}}}\right),

for some constant
C>0
. Additionally, all the results in subsection
4.3
can be adapted to this case.

Proof.

It suffices to verify this case for the parabolic problem (which is the hardest case). Furthermore, the only key result required to be
established is the validity of Theorem
6
; in particular, the derivation of the results in the first step of the proof for this
particular case. Select in this case

\hat{k}^{2}_{0}:=\left\{\displaystyle\int^{T}_{0}\left[\mathcal{M}^{\tilde{p}}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\tilde{f}(t),\Omega)\right]^{\kappa_{\tilde{p}}}dt\right\}^{2/\kappa_{\tilde{p}}}+\left\{\displaystyle\int^{T}_{0}\left[\mathcal{N}^{\tilde{q}}_{\eta_{\tilde{q}}}(\tilde{g}(t),\Gamma,\mu)\right]^{\kappa_{\tilde{q}}}dt\right\}^{2/\kappa_{\tilde{q}}},

where we recall that
\eta_{\tilde{q}}:=1-(N-d)/\tilde{q}
.
Then, for
k\geq\hat{k}_{0}
, after a verification of the behavior of the nonlocal integral representations for

(\mathcal{J}^{{}_{B}}_{{}_{\Omega}}\tilde{f}(t,x))u_{k}
and
(\Theta^{{}_{B}}_{{}_{\Gamma}}\tilde{g}(t,x))u_{k}
,
proceeding in the exact way as in the proof of the first step in the proof, we arrive at the following inequality:

\Upsilon^{2}_{\varsigma}(\psi u_{k})\,\leq\,c\left\{L_{\psi^{\prime}}\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}\|u_{k}(t)\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega_{k}(t)}}\,dt\right.+

+k^{2}\left(\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}\left(\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}(t)}\int_{\Omega_{k}(t)}\frac{|\tilde{f}(t,x)-\tilde{f}(t,y)|^{2}}{k^{2}|x-y|^{2\mathfrak{s}+2N/\tilde{p}}}\,dxdy+\right.\right.

+\left.\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}(t)}\int_{\Omega\setminus\Omega_{k}(t)}\frac{|\tilde{f}(t,x)-\tilde{f}(t,y)|^{2}}{k^{2}|x-y|^{2\mathfrak{s}+2N/\tilde{p}}}\,dxdy+\displaystyle\int_{\Omega\setminus\Omega_{k}(t)}\int_{\Omega_{k}(t)}\frac{|\tilde{f}(t,x)-\tilde{f}(t,y)|^{2}}{k^{2}|x-y|^{2\mathfrak{s}+2N/\tilde{p}}}\,dxdy\right)dt+

\,\,\,\,\,+\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}\left(\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma_{k}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{k}(t)}\frac{|\tilde{g}(t,x)-\tilde{g}(t,y)|^{2}}{k^{2}|x-y|^{2+(4d-2N)/\tilde{q}}}\,d\mu_{x}d\mu_{y}+\right.

+\left.\left.\left.\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma_{k}(t)}\int_{\Gamma\setminus\Gamma_{k}(t)}\frac{|\tilde{g}(t,x)-\tilde{g}(t,y)|^{2}}{k^{2}|x-y|^{2+(4d-2N)/\tilde{q}}}\,d\mu_{x}d\mu_{y}+\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma\setminus\Gamma_{k}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{k}(t)}\frac{|\tilde{g}(t,x)-\tilde{g}(t,y)|^{2}}{k^{2}|x-y|^{2+(4d-2N)/\tilde{q}}}\,d\mu_{x}d\mu_{y}\right)dt\right)\right\},

(7.16)

for some constant
c>0
(which can vary from line to line), where as before
L_{\psi^{\prime}}:=\displaystyle\sup_{-\varsigma\leq t\leq 0}|\psi^{\prime}(t)|
.
Estimating the nonlocal-type data terms, we calculate and obtain that

\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}\left\{\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}(t)}\int_{\Omega_{k}(t)}\frac{|\tilde{f}(t,x)-\tilde{f}(t,y)|^{2}}{k^{2}|x-y|^{2\mathfrak{s}+2N/\tilde{p}}}\,dxdy+\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{k}(t)}\int_{\Omega\setminus\Omega_{k}(t)}\frac{|\tilde{f}(t,x)-\tilde{f}(t,y)|^{2}}{k^{2}|x-y|^{2\mathfrak{s}+2N/\tilde{p}}}\,dxdy+\right.

\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\left.+\displaystyle\int_{\Omega\setminus\Omega_{k}(t)}\int_{\Omega^{\pm}_{k}(t)}\frac{|\tilde{f}(t,x)-\tilde{f}(t,y)|^{2}}{k^{2}|x-y|^{2\mathfrak{s}+2N/\tilde{p}}}\,dxdy\right\}dt

\leq\,\displaystyle\frac{3}{k^{2}}|\Omega|^{1/\breve{\tilde{p}}}\left\{\displaystyle\int^{T}_{0}\left[\mathcal{M}^{\tilde{p}}_{\mathfrak{s}}(\tilde{f}(t),\Omega)\right]^{\kappa_{\tilde{p}}}dt\right\}^{1/\kappa_{\tilde{p}}}\left(\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}|\Omega_{k}(t)|^{\breve{\kappa}_{\tilde{p}}/\breve{\tilde{p}}}dt\right)^{1/\breve{\kappa}_{\tilde{p}}}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,

\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\leq\,3|\Omega|^{1/\breve{\tilde{p}}}\left(\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}|\Omega_{k}(t)|^{\breve{\kappa}_{\tilde{p}}/\breve{\tilde{p}}}dt\right)^{1/\breve{\kappa}_{\tilde{p}}},

(7.17)

and

\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}\left\{\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma_{k}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{k}(t)}\frac{|\tilde{g}(t,x)-\tilde{g}(t,y)|^{2}}{k^{2}|x-y|^{2+(4d-2N)/\tilde{q}}}\,d\mu_{x}d\mu_{y}+\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma_{k}(t)}\int_{\Gamma\setminus\Gamma_{k}(t)}\frac{|\tilde{g}(t,x)-\tilde{g}(t,y)|^{2}}{k^{2}|x-y|^{2+(4d-2N)/\tilde{q}}}\,d\mu_{x}d\mu_{y}+\right.

\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\left.+\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma\setminus\Gamma_{k}(t)}\int_{\Gamma_{k}(t)}\frac{|\tilde{g}(t,x)-\tilde{g}(t,y)|^{2}}{k^{2}|x-y|^{2+(4d-2N)/\tilde{q}}}\,d\mu_{x}d\mu_{y}\right\}d\xi,

\leq\,\displaystyle\frac{3}{k^{2}}\mu(\Gamma)^{1/\breve{\tilde{q}}}\left\{\displaystyle\int^{T}_{0}\left[\mathcal{N}^{\tilde{q}}_{\eta_{\tilde{q}}}(\tilde{g}(t),\Gamma,\mu)\right]^{\kappa_{\tilde{q}}}dt\right\}^{1/\kappa_{\tilde{q}}}\left(\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}\mu(\Gamma_{k}(t))^{\breve{\kappa}_{\tilde{q}}/\breve{\tilde{q}}}dt\right)^{1/\breve{\kappa}_{\tilde{q}}}\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,

\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\leq\,3\mu(\Gamma)^{1/\breve{\tilde{q}}}\left(\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}\mu(\Gamma_{k}(t))^{\breve{\kappa}_{\tilde{q}}/\breve{\tilde{q}}}dt\right)^{1/\breve{\kappa}_{\tilde{q}}},

(7.18)

where
\breve{\tau^{\prime}}:=2\tau^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime}-2)^{-1}
for
\tau^{\prime}\in(2,\infty)
. Then letting

\theta_{1,1}:=\frac{2N+2\mathfrak{r}-N\mathfrak{r}}{N\mathfrak{r}},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,r_{1,1}:=2(1+\theta_{1,1}),\,\,\,\,\,\,\,s_{1,1}:=(1+\theta_{1,1})\mathfrak{r},

\theta_{1,2}:=\frac{4\breve{\tilde{p}}+4\breve{\kappa}_{\tilde{p}}N-\breve{\tilde{p}}\breve{\kappa}_{\tilde{p}}N}{\breve{\tilde{p}}\breve{\kappa}_{\tilde{p}}N},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,r_{1,2}:=(1+\theta_{1,2})\breve{\kappa}_{\tilde{p}},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,s_{1,2}:=(1+\theta_{1,2})\breve{\tilde{p}},

\theta_{2,1}:=\frac{2d+2\mathfrak{s}-N\mathfrak{s}}{N\mathfrak{s}},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,r_{2,1}:=2(1+\theta_{2,1}),\,\,\,\,\,\,\,s_{2,1}:=(1+\theta_{2,1})\mathfrak{s},

\theta_{2,2}:=\frac{4\breve{\tilde{q}}+4\breve{\kappa}_{\tilde{q}}d-\breve{\tilde{q}}\breve{\kappa}_{\tilde{q}}N}{\breve{\tilde{q}}\breve{\kappa}_{\tilde{q}}N},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,r_{2,2}:=(1+\theta_{2,2})\breve{\kappa}_{\tilde{q}},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,s_{2,2}:=(1+\theta_{2,2})\breve{\tilde{q}},

we get that

\Upsilon^{2}_{\varsigma}(\psi u_{k})\,\leq\,cL_{\psi^{\prime}}\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}\|u_{k}(t)\|^{2}_{{}_{2,\Omega_{k}(t)}}\,dt+

+ck^{2}\displaystyle\sum^{2}_{\xi=1}\left(\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}|\Omega_{k}(t)|^{{}^{\frac{r_{1,\xi}}{s_{1,\xi}}}}\,dt\right)^{{}^{\frac{2(1+\theta_{1,\xi})}{r_{1,\xi}}}}+ck^{2}\displaystyle\sum^{2}_{\zeta=1}\left(\displaystyle\int^{0}_{-\varsigma}\mu(\Gamma_{k}(t))^{{}^{\frac{r_{2,\zeta}}{s_{2,\zeta}}}}\,dt\right)^{{}^{\frac{2(1+\theta_{2,\zeta})}{r_{2,\zeta}}}}.

(7.19)

From here, we proceed exactly as in the proof of the first step in Theorem
6
, and the rest
of the proofs run unchanged. This is all what we need to verify, so we are done.

\Box

Remark 9
.

In Theorem
17
(b), observe that we are not guaranteeing the existence of a weak solution, as the procedure in subsection
4.2
may not be applicable for nonlocal data as in problem (
7.15
). The crucial change is that for this nonlocal version of the problem, the operator
\mathbf{A}
defined in Definition
6
(d) needs to be extended to the space
H^{-\mathfrak{s}/2}(\Omega)\times H^{-r_{d}/2}(\Gamma)
. Since it is unknown whether the dual of the fractional Sobolev space possesses order continuous norm (see
[
11
, Appendix C]
for a precise definition), it is unknown whether
\mathbf{A}
may generate a once integrated semigroup (Cf.
[
11
, Theorem 3.11.7]
), and thus Proposition
3
may not hold in this case. In the case
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are symmetric of Laplace-type structure, an alternative path has been recently considered in
[
48
]
.

7.4.
Applications of nonlocal maps

The presence of nonlocal boundary value problems have experienced a substantial growth in interest and investigation, motivated by
many applications and advances in the field. For instance, nonlocal Besov-type operators as in subsection
7.1
are
important maps to showcase the presence of “fractional” diffusion along
\Omega
(in the case of the map
\mathcal{J}_{{}_{\Omega}}

given by (
7.1
)), and over
\Gamma
(in the case of the map
\Theta_{{}_{\Gamma}}
given by (
7.1
)).
In fact, the inclusion of these kind of nonlocal maps provide a bridge to consider the realization of fractional-type boundary value problems
involving unbounded coefficients over rough domains, which is rather new as of the present time (refer to
[
99
]
in the case of bounded coefficients).
As a more concrete example, we recall the classes of ramified domains given in subsection
5.3
, which are useful in the simulation
of the diffusion of medical sprays in the bronchial tree. Then, since oxygen diffusion between the lungs and the circulatory system takes place
only in the last generation of the lungs tree, a reasonable diffusion model may need to involve nonlocal Robin boundary conditions containing
the Besov boundary operator
\Theta_{{}_{\Gamma}}
given by (
7.1
). The Besov nonlocal maps are also of great value to establish a transition
between nonlocal diffusion equations and the corresponding nonlocal diffusion processes which could be of great value in applications to stable-like processes
(see for example
[
19
,
30
]
).

The Dirichlet-to-Neumann map has been a very useful tool to deal with multiple inverse problems, and has also fundamental in the study of
some models related to electrical impedance tomography and conductivity (e.g.
[
55
,
56
,
91
]
). Recently in
[
40
]
,
the authors developed a model relying in the Dirichlet-to-Neumann map to deal a low-frequency electromagnetic modeling which is computationally
accurate and efficient. Furthermore, in
[
52
,
73
]
, the Dirichlet-to-Neumann map has been a fundamental tool used to develop an
efficient numerical method for the modal analysis of two-dimensional photonic crystal waves (in the area of optics).

In views of the above applications, we take the opportunity to comment on the conclusion of Theorem
17
, which establishes
the existence and regularity theory of problems (
7.14
) and (
7.15
). In these boundary value problems, the inhomogeneous interior and boundary
data are given in terms of the nonlocal Besov operators defined in subsection
7.1
, which correspond to interesting examples of
boundary value problems with “fractional-type” data. These kind of model equations are rather new, especially in the time-dependent setting,
and provide the ground for further analysis and applications of problems with fractional data.
